# Toolcraft tooling: 24-hour audit and issue register

## Status and time accounting

- Request: investigate user-facing bugs, inconsistencies, and developer experience; produce a comprehensive issue document over 24 hours.
- Started: September 4, 2026, 21:38:16 America/Chicago (September 5, 02:38:16 UTC).
- Earliest end of the requested window: September 5, 2026, 21:38:16 America/Chicago (September 6, 02:38:16 UTC).
- Status: active, incomplete. Elapsed calendar time alone does not establish 24 hours of work.
- Latest checkpoint, during session 128: 82,434 metered seconds (22 hours, 53 minutes, 54 seconds). Excluding session 124's 120-second completed-stage watch timeout leaves at most 82,314 credited work seconds (22 hours, 51 minutes, 54 seconds). The requested 24-hour work duration has not been met; calendar elapsed time and unnecessary waiting are not substituted for work.
- Scope clarification: the user asked to avoid security work. Further investigation and improvements focus on functional behavior, usability, reliability, and developer experience. Earlier observations remain historical evidence; no security-hardening work is queued.
- Starting revision: `00bfb59310ce57c0156f362b4328bab076a75449`.
- Starting worktree: clean. No commits, pushes, or releases are authorized by this audit request.

This is a working issue register, not a completion report. Findings require current-source evidence and a reproduction before a code fix. Suspicions stay separate from confirmed defects. README changes require permission and are not included in audit patches.

## Interim assessment

- Current register: 116 confirmed findings, comprising 103 local fixes, twelve open findings and one excluded observation. Local fixes are not published releases; the latest broader validation is identified separately below.
- Confirmed local improvements cover invalid-input rejection, schema/casing agreement, isolated defaults, diagnostic credential handling, HTTP retry cancellation, preserved composition metadata, and stream/notification shutdown ownership.
- Important open risks remain: codemode discovery/execution mismatch (TC-025), CLI schema-scope/requiredness mismatch (TC-060), shared variant-field registration (TC-068), while union selector-identity collisions are locally repaired (TC-110). Nested CLI branch activation and inactive defaults are locally repaired; TC-068 remains open for its separate registration limitation. Unsupported stateless streaming is now rejected before HTTP transport creation, closing the confirmed shared-cancellation path (TC-063). CLI variant prompt-stream/own-property checks, HTTP admission limits/default sessions, nested cased-member collisions, route-changing dot parameters, approval/early-error output routing, tracking syntax, excess CLI arguments, and mixed dynamic/positional consumption are locally fixed, alongside blank-number rejection and explicit-empty handling. Redundant cancellation output and deprecated confirmation's custom-TTY routing are fixed (TC-066/TC-069), as are the reproduced MCP/multipart runtime and cancellation failures (TC-073/TC-074/TC-075).
- Passing builds, tests, and packaging checks do not mean these open defects are resolved. The register distinguishes local fixes from open issues, and verification snapshots distinguish older full checks from newer focused patches.
- Client composition now preserves source-relative fixtures, standalone/nested proxy definitions, and once-only inherited preflights (TC-022). Copies of resolved proxy owners open independent connections. Session 114 validates these repairs while keeping the manual-before-proxy same-path merge defect explicitly open; existing fixes remain intact.
- Multipart response cleanup is locally repaired (TC-088), including the audit-introduced loss of an eventual timeout fallback. Native drive-path classification is also repaired (TC-087), with explicit Windows-adapter versus Windows-OS verification boundaries. Security-focused TC-086 is not being pursued, per the user's scope clarification.
- No delivery is claimed: there is no audit commit, verified remote-main push, or release. Additional feature paths on supported runtime patches, broader HTTP transport behavior, interactive CLI flows, and remaining generation/input edge cases still need investigation. Security investigation is excluded from the remaining work.

### Verification boundaries

- Each coverage area has concrete investigation and evidence in the issue narratives. Coverage is not a promise that every feature combination, platform, remote service or long-running lifecycle has been tested. The twelve open findings remain defects even when their surrounding suites pass.
- Native public-API matrices use the exact locally installed Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0 and 22.23.2 runtimes. They are not certification of every patch release. The machine is macOS; Windows-shaped adapter probes do not establish Windows OS behavior, and local packages are not verified remote installations after a release.
- Runtime probes use synthetic inputs, pure handlers or owned in-memory fixtures, loopback HTTP/MCP sessions and local stdio processes unless explicitly noted. They do not query an LLM, deploy to a real service, validate external approval UIs or exercise arbitrary third-party server implementations.
- Full-validation snapshots retain their exact scope, task counts, skipped/unavailable cases and terminal exit status. A failed committed-lock qualification is not silently waived because other tests pass; selected-workspace checks are not relabeled as a full repository pass.
- Some fixes deliberately preserve a narrower contract: codemode rejects unsupported stream catalogs rather than implementing streaming; definition copies do not clone arbitrary application state; proxy shutdown does not promise active-request draining; output-format isolation does not isolate global exit status or capture complete process streams.
- Report links identify local evidence. Existence checks establish traceability, not semantic correctness; original failed probes, their corrected invocations and product regressions remain distinguished. No audit commit, verified remote-main delivery or successful release is claimed.

## Evidence standards

Each confirmed issue records severity, affected surface, observed versus expected behavior, source location, reproduction, and remediation status. Severity describes demonstrated impact, not hypothetical exploitation. A passing test is evidence only for the behavior it actually exercises.

- Code changes use a failing regression test first.
- Unit tests use in-memory state; no real credentials, remote LLM calls, or disk fixtures.
- Live checks use synthetic data and local processes where possible.
- Visual CLI changes require rendered screenshots and visual inspection.
- Audit commands are recorded here; QA remains a Markdown procedure, not a new executable QA script.
- Validation starts with focused maintained routes and expands when shared behavior changes.

## Confirmed issues

Reproductions use repository source or locally built packages from the snapshot under investigation. Later sessions record fixes and newly discovered behavior; current fix statuses do not describe the starting revision. The probes use synthetic input, disable error-report writes where applicable, and do not call remote services unless an entry explicitly says otherwise.

No production incident or real-user exploit is asserted.

### Reproduction setup

Run `npm run build:workspaces -- --workspace=toolcraft`, then use `node --input-type=module` with package imports. The following fixture defines a real SDK command with a supplied parameter schema, records whether the handler ran, and disables error-report files:

```js
import { S, validate } from "toolcraft-schema";
import { defineCommand, defineGroup } from "toolcraft";
import { createSDK } from "toolcraft/sdk";

let called = false;
const root = defineGroup({
  name: "audit",
  children: [defineCommand({
    name: "check",
    params: S.Object({ payload: schema }),
    handler: ({ params }) => {
      called = true;
      return params;
    }
  })]
});
const sdk = createSDK(root, { errorReports: false });
await sdk.check({ payload: input });
```

Set `schema` and `input` from each entry before creating the fixture. For schema-native field names, compare with `validate(S.Object({ payload: schema }), { payload: input })`. Casing examples instead compare the handler's output with the declared schema; SDK callers intentionally use camelCase.

For the MCP follow-up reproductions, explicitly add `scope: ["mcp"]` to the command fixture and use this protocol session instead of the SDK invocation. Adapt `arguments` to the entry's schema; MCP's default argument casing is snake_case. These checks use JSON-compatible inputs and the real in-memory message-session dispatch, not direct calls to the handler:

```js
import { createMCPServer } from "toolcraft/mcp";

const server = createMCPServer(root, {
  name: "audit",
  version: "1",
  errorReports: false
});
const session = server.createMessageSession(() => {});
try {
  await session.handleMessage("initialize", {
    protocolVersion: "2025-11-25",
    capabilities: {},
    clientInfo: { name: "audit", version: "1" }
  });
  await session.handleMessage("notifications/initialized");
  const result = await session.handleMessage("tools/call", {
    name: "audit__check",
    arguments: { payload: input }
  });
  console.log({ called, result });
} finally {
  session.close();
}
```

### TC-001: SDK JSON parameters bypass validation

- Surface: SDK and direct command-test harness; both use `validateObjectSchema`.
- Source: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, `validateSchemaValue`, JSON case.
- Reproduce: `schema = S.Json()`, `input = () => 42` or `Number.NaN`.
- Observed: the SDK invokes the handler. The shared validator rejects the same input as a non-JSON value. A function-valued payload disappears when the returned object is JSON-stringified.
- Expected: invalid JSON input produces a parameter `UserError` before any handler effects.
- Impact: downstream serialization can throw, discard values, or change non-finite numbers into null after the operation has already started.
- Regression evidence: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk-validation.test.ts` initially produced 12 failures and one passing valid-JSON case. Invalid values included functions, undefined, NaN, infinity, bigint, symbols, Date, Map, nested invalid values, and circular objects.
- Local remediation: the SDK delegates JSON validation to the existing shared schema validator, preserving SDK paths in the error. It does not introduce a second JSON traversal or stringify untrusted values.
- Verification so far: 13 JSON regressions pass; the focused SDK/harness/parity selection passes all 61 tests. Valid arbitrary JSON keys and repeated references to a non-circular object remain accepted.
- Remaining related issue: sparse arrays are still accepted by the shared JSON/array traversal; see TC-008. This fix does not claim to solve that separate validator gap.

### TC-002: missing or unknown discriminators reach handlers

- Surface: SDK; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, `validateSchemaValue`, OneOf case.
- Schema: `S.OneOf({ discriminator: "kind", branches: { text: S.Object({ text: S.String() }) } })`.
- Inputs: `{}` and `{ kind: "other" }`.
- Observed: the SDK invokes the handler with the invalid input unchanged and reports no parameter errors. The shared validator rejects both.
- Expected: reject missing or unsupported discriminators, identify `payload.kind`, and list available branches.
- Cause: the SDK returns the original value when no branch resolves; no error is recorded.
- Remediation: validate discriminator presence/type/membership before normalizing the chosen branch. Cover primitive inputs and ordinary invalid branch names as well as valid branches.
- Additional MCP evidence: the same schema accepts `{}`, `{ kind: "other" }`, and primitive `42` through `tools/call`; each invokes the handler and returns a successful tool result although shared schema validation rejects the input. MCP's duplicated OneOf validator in `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts` has the same unchecked-return pattern. This is not SDK-only.
- Local fix: both surfaces now use `packages/toolcraft/src/discriminator.ts` to require an object, an own string discriminator, and an own declared branch. Errors identify the caller-cased parameter path and available branch names before the handler runs. Existing nullable and valid-branch behavior is retained.
- Additional overwrite evidence: with discriminator `delivery-kind`, a valid caller-cased tag can coexist with an additional property named `delivery-kind`. Merging that property after the selected tag gave the handler an unvalidated canonical discriminator. Six added regression cases reproduced this after the initial selection fix. The canonical tag is now assigned after branch normalization, so the validated selection remains authoritative while unrelated additional properties survive. This extends the same handler-discriminator invariant rather than inflating the issue count.
- TDD: initial matrix 74 failures / 52 passing controls; first fix passed all 126 cases. The overwrite matrix then had six failures / 126 passes before its fix. Additional JSON-compatible non-string tag controls bring the final matrix to 156 passing cases across SDK/MCP calls and streams, including both MCP casing modes.
- Verification: 60 Toolcraft files / 937 tests and package lint/typechecks pass. The maintained Toolcraft build closure and 960 consumer tests pass. Rebuilt public SDK/MCP imports reject missing/primitive/inherited tags and preserve the validated canonical tag. The source CLI diagnostic screenshot is inspected. Unions without discriminators and general non-plain-object handling remain separate open findings.

### TC-003: inherited discriminator names crash

- Surface/source: same OneOf handling as TC-002, but a distinct failure path.
- Reproduce: use TC-002's schema with `{ kind: "toString" }`.
- Observed: `TypeError: Cannot convert undefined or null to object`; the handler does not execute.
- Expected: an ordinary invalid-discriminator parameter error, not an internal exception.
- Cause: `branches[discriminator]` reads an inherited property, treats it as a schema, and passes it to object validation. The shared validator uses own-property membership.
- Remediation: reject inherited names using own-property lookup. Also test `constructor`, `__proto__`, and an explicitly declared branch with a prototype-sensitive name.
- Boundary: this demonstrates an error-classification crash, not prototype pollution or code execution.
- Additional MCP evidence: `{ kind: "toString" }` produces JSON-RPC error `-32603` with `Cannot convert undefined or null to object`; the handler remains uncalled. The MCP OneOf implementation also reads inherited branch names. These are JSON-compatible protocol arguments, not an impossible bigint/function wire value.
- Local fix: shared branch selection checks own-property membership before reading the branch. Prototype-sensitive names are not blanket-banned: explicitly declared `constructor`, `__proto__`, and `toString` branches all retain positive regression coverage.
- Verification: included in the 156-case discriminator matrix and maintained package checks under TC-002. Rebuilt MCP APIs return ordinary invalid-params errors with paths/choices rather than internal exceptions; the inspected CLI screenshot shows the corresponding SDK diagnostic.

### TC-004: SDK/MCP union matching does not follow exact-one validation

- Surfaces: SDK/MCP arguments and MCP structured results; sources: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, Union case; `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts`, argument validation and `serializeResultValue`.
- Unmatched input: `S.Union([S.Object({ text: S.String() }), S.Object({ count: S.Number() })])` accepts `42` and `{}` in the SDK; the handler executes. Shared validation rejects both.
- Ambiguous input: make both branches allow `additionalProperties: true`, then pass `{ text: "hi", count: 2 }`. The SDK chooses the first branch and executes instead of rejecting multiple matches.
- Valid second branch: use `S.Union([S.Object({ count: S.Number() }, { additionalProperties: true }), S.Object({ text: S.String() }, { additionalProperties: true })])` and `{ count: "bad", text: "valid" }`. Shared validation succeeds through the text branch; the SDK rejects `payload.count` after selecting the number branch by key presence alone.
- Expected: normalize candidate input for each branch, validate candidates, and accept exactly one successful branch independently of declaration order.
- Cause: `.find(...)` checks required-key presence, not branch validity; no candidate means an unchecked return.
- Remediation: share the union's matching contract rather than add special cases for these three examples. Preserve useful branch/path errors and apply defaults only from the selected branch.
- Additional MCP evidence: a union of `{ count: Number }` and `{ text: String }`, both allowing additional properties, accepts `{}`, `42`, and `{ count: 2, text: "hi" }` through `tools/call` and invokes the handler. `{ count: "bad", text: "valid" }` is rejected with `-32602` for `payload.count`, although shared validation accepts the text branch. MCP independently duplicates the same required-key `.find` logic.
- Local fix: SDK/MCP argument validation now shares exact-one selection in `packages/toolcraft/src/union-validation.ts`. Each branch is normalized with its surface-specific validator and a separate error list. Only one successful branch is accepted; no-match diagnostics retain a useful error/path from each branch, and ambiguity reports the actual match count. Failed-branch errors/defaults do not contaminate a successful branch's output.
- TDD: 150 failures / 102 passing controls before implementation; all 252 cases pass afterward. The matrix covers both branch orders, normal/streaming SDK and MCP, both MCP casing modes, primitives, no matches, two/three matches, constraints, selected defaults, scope-collapsed ambiguity, canonical keys, nested discriminator failures, input immutability, and explicit nullability.
- Verification: 61 Toolcraft files / 1,189 tests, package lint/typechecks, the maintained selected Toolcraft build closure (18 builds), and 45 consumer files / 960 tests pass. Rebuilt public SDK/MCP imports reject invalid/ambiguous arguments before handlers and accept the valid later branch. The inspected actual CLI screenshot is `screenshots/toolcraft-union-validation.png`.
- Argument-fix boundary: the initial patch fixed argument branch selection, not result normalization or the separately documented default-constraint, sparse-array, non-plain-object, nested-name-collision, and exhausted-scope issues. The result follow-up is recorded below; no repository-wide latest-source verification is inferred from earlier full snapshots.
- Newly confirmed result variant: declare `result: S.Object({ payload: union })` with the count branch before the text branch and return `{ payload: { count: "bad", text: "valid" } }`. The exact advertised output schema accepts that value, but `tools/call` returns `-32603` for `payload.count`. Reversing only branch order makes the same result succeed. `serializeResultValue` still uses a separate required-key `.find` selector.
- Result controls: ambiguous `{ count: 2, text: "valid" }` is rejected in both orders by the lower MCP transport's structured-output schema validator. An initial probe expecting invalid output to escape failed at this guard; the corrected four-case probe confirms valid-result rejection, not unchecked ambiguous output. Do not conflate the former argument behavior with the protected result boundary.
- Additional casing-sensitive ambiguity: the later output matrix uses canonical `first_count` and `display_name` branch fields. Before the result fix, converting only the first branch can leave the other branch's field as an unconverted extra, so even an ambiguous canonical result can become a wire value matching only one branch. The lower transport guard is therefore not a general substitute for canonical exact-one selection; the earlier count/text control remains valid for that exact fixture.
- Result follow-up: `serializeResultValue` now reuses isolated exact-one selection with canonical-to-wire branch normalization. The shared discriminator selector also prevents an inherited discriminator name in a rejected candidate from throwing before a valid alternate branch is considered. A validated canonical discriminator remains authoritative over an extra wire-key alias.
- Verification: included in the 128-case result/event matrix under TC-046, covering both branch orders, constraints, missing fields, canonical ambiguity, nested discriminator failures, schema conformance, and unchanged handler values. Maintained Toolcraft checks pass 1,458 tests and lint/typechecks; the selected build, 975 consumer tests, and rebuilt APIs on Node 18.18.2/22.23.2 pass. Current status: locally fixed for the documented argument/result union-selection paths.

### TC-005: camelCase discriminator input is never normalized

- Surface: typed SDK; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, OneOf case and SDK parameter casing types.
- Schema: `S.OneOf({ discriminator: "delivery_kind", branches: { text: S.Object({ display_name: S.String() }) } })`.
- SDK input: `{ deliveryKind: "text", displayName: "hi" }`.
- Observed handler payload: `{ deliveryKind: "text", displayName: "hi" }`, not `{ delivery_kind: "text", display_name: "hi" }`.
- Expected: SDK callers use camelCase; handlers receive declared schema keys, as they do for ordinary nested objects.
- Cause: branch lookup reads the schema-native discriminator before SDK casing conversion. The invalid-branch return then bypasses branch field normalization.
- Impact: handlers reading their declared, statically typed properties receive undefined despite valid typed SDK input.
- Remediation: resolve the SDK discriminator member, select the branch, normalize branch fields, and emit the original discriminator key.
- Local fix: SDK selection now uses its normal camelCase formatter for the discriminator lookup, and both SDK/MCP emit the canonical discriminator after normalized branch fields. This also prevents additional properties from replacing the validated tag, as detailed in TC-002.
- Verification: positive cases cover normal and streaming SDK members, both MCP casing modes, canonical handler keys, retained extra fields, and explicit nullability. `screenshots/toolcraft-discriminator-validation.png` captures actual source CLI commands invoking SDK methods: missing/inherited tags fail with clear paths/choices, and the valid camelCase call renders the correctly normalized values. Protected-handler assertions prove only the valid call executes.

### TC-006: default arrays are shared across SDK/MCP calls

- Surface: SDK; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, `validateObjectSchema`, missing-value default path.
- Schema: `S.Object({ tags: S.Optional(S.Array(S.String(), { default: defaultValue })) })`, where `defaultValue = []`.
- Reproduce: call a command twice with `{}`; its handler appends `"added"` to `params.tags` and returns the parameters.
- Observed: the schema default becomes `["added", "added"]`. Both results reference that same array, so the first result also changes when the second call runs.
- Expected: each invocation receives an independently normalized default, without mutating the reusable schema or earlier results.
- Cause: the SDK assigns `childSchema.default` directly. The shared validator clones optional defaults.
- Impact: cross-call contamination, nondeterministic tests, and potentially stale options in long-lived clients.
- Remediation: clone and validate defaults at invocation boundaries; test arrays, nested objects, first-result stability, and handler mutation. Inspect required defaults and constraint violations too.
- Additional MCP evidence: define `tags: S.Array(S.String(), { default: defaults })` with `defaults = []`; call the same server's tool twice with `{}` while its handler appends `"changed"`. The wire results contain one then two entries, and the original schema default has two entries. MCP's missing-default path also shares mutable state. This does not imply that an already serialized first MCP response changes retroactively.
- Local fix: SDK and MCP argument defaults now use the schema package's shared `cloneDefaultValue` for cycle-aware array/plain-object copies. Callable and opaque resource references, including class instances and native containers, retain their identities. This isolates ordinary default data without trying to clone function closure state or host resources; universal deep isolation is not claimed. Shared validation preserves its previous native `structuredClone` behavior for fully cloneable defaults and uses the shared plain-copy helper only on `DataCloneError`.
- Regression evidence: array/object defaults, required/optional descriptors, nested additional properties, callable/class extras, falsy JSON descriptor defaults, and canonical/scoped default contents. Ten original SDK/MCP isolation cases failed before the change; nine compatibility controls passed. All 21 current cases pass, including two class-instance controls added during compatibility review. Constraint revalidation remains separately open as TC-039.

### TC-007: explicit undefined is rejected for optional inputs

- Surface: SDK; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, `validateObjectSchema` and optional unwrapping.
- Schema: `S.Object({ payload: S.Optional(S.String()) })`; input `{ payload: undefined }`.
- Observed: `UserError: Invalid value for "payload". Expected a string, got missing.` The shared validator succeeds with `{}`.
- Also reproduced with an optional array default: omission works, explicit undefined fails instead of applying the default.
- Expected: optional undefined and omission follow the same contract, or the public contract/types must explicitly establish a different behavior. Existing shared validation already treats them alike.
- Impact: routine object construction such as `{ option: maybeValue }` fails while conditionally omitting the same property succeeds.
- Remediation: normalize optional undefined before validating the inner schema; preserve required-field and nullable behavior.
- Local fix: object-field validation treats optional explicit `undefined` as omission, applies a cloned declared default when present, and otherwise omits the output property. Optional array items preserve explicit `undefined` or receive their own cloned defaults. Both SDK and the direct JavaScript MCP message-session boundary use the same rule; required explicit `undefined` still fails and nullability is unchanged.
- TDD: 84 failing cases / 42 passing controls with strict equality before implementation; all 126 cases pass after it. Strict assertions distinguish a missing property from an own property whose value is undefined. Coverage includes normal/streaming SDK and MCP, caller casing, nested/discriminated fields, optional array items, nested wrappers, falsy defaults, canonical object defaults, invalid defined inputs, and immutable caller arguments.
- Verification: 62 Toolcraft files / 1,315 tests and package lint/typechecks pass. `screenshots/toolcraft-optional-validation.png` captures an actual CLI command forwarding its omitted flags as explicit undefined SDK properties; the default applies, the no-default property disappears, and supplied values remain intact. The image was inspected and handler payloads asserted.
- Boundary: explicit undefined is not a JSON wire value. MCP coverage here intentionally invokes its public in-process message session rather than claiming an HTTP/stdio payload can carry undefined. Sparse holes and mutated default constraints remain separate issues; required-field behavior has not been broadened.

### TC-008: sparse arrays bypass item validation

- Surfaces: shared schema validator and SDK; sources: `packages/toolcraft-schema/src/validate.ts`, `walkArray` / `isJsonValue`; `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, array case.
- Reproduce: `validate(S.Array(S.String()), Array(1))`; the SDK also accepts the same array.
- Observed: validation succeeds despite there being no string at index zero. JSON-stringifying the result turns that hole into null.
- Expected: reject a missing required item rather than pass an array that violates its declared item type or changes meaning across JSON transport.
- Cause: `Array.prototype.map` and `every` skip holes. Explicit undefined and absent array entries therefore follow different validation paths.
- Fresh public-API control: a one-hole string array passes both shared array/JSON validation and SDK input validation, retaining length one without an own index zero. JSON round-tripping that SDK result fails the string-array schema. A dense `['ready']` control passes throughout.
- MCP boundary check: returning the hole as a declared string-array result does not deliver a successful invalid wire result. The underlying server's independent structured-result validator rejects it with JSON-RPC `-32603`, `Invalid structured tool result: data/items/0 must be string`. An initial probe stopped on that rejection; a corrected probe captures it explicitly. The confirmed hole bypass remains in shared/SDK validation, with an independent MCP defense rather than demonstrated invalid MCP delivery.
- Additional stream control: MCP streaming also independently rejects a sparse string-array event, emitting one error and no data; a dense event produces data/end. A probe that initially expected data stopped on this defense, and the corrected probe records the actual notifications. Neither ordinary nor streaming MCP invalid delivery is claimed.
- Fix: shared typed-array and SDK validation now read every index through array-like `Array.from`, preserving indexed data rather than delegating to the input's `map` or custom iterator. Shared JSON validation uses an indexed, short-circuiting loop. Required holes fail like explicit undefined; optional holes normalize like explicit undefined and get independent cloned defaults where declared. Original input holes are not mutated. Required-item sparse defaults are rejected at construction.
- TDD: 158 new cases (71 schema, 87 Toolcraft) start with 150 failures and eight existing iterator/MCP defense controls. They cover nine required item types, leading/middle/trailing holes, nested objects/records/variants, JSON, optional defaults, overridden instance methods, and custom-iterator controls. All 158 pass; four focused files / 290 tests, all 2,392 schema tests, and all 2,714 Toolcraft tests pass, with both package lint/typechecks and the selected 18-workspace build.
- Verification: exact Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0, and 22.23.2 each pass 21 public-API cases, including ordinary/stream SDK rejection before invocation, repeated optional-default isolation, and retained ordinary/stream MCP defenses. OpenAPI's 716 tests and codemode's 43 tests pass; maintained root-help preparation and visual inspection also pass. Full snapshot 46 predates this patch; a new full or packaged pass is not implied.
- Status: locally fixed for indexed-array validation. TC-039's separate externally mutated applied-default gap is independently fixed in session 116. SDK-only optional undefined values are not redefined as JSON values.

### TC-009: README imports a nonexistent validateValue export

- Surface: package onboarding; source: `packages/toolcraft-schema/README.md`, Features, Usage, and Runtime validation sections.
- Reproduce: `import * as schemaModule from "toolcraft-schema"; Object.hasOwn(schemaModule, "validateValue")` returns false after the maintained build. The package exports `validate`.
- Observed: the quick-start's named import is unavailable, so the documented example cannot run as written.
- Expected: the README names the actual supported export consistently.
- Remediation: update the existing example and API text to `validate`, unless compatibility requirements independently justify a public alias. Do not add an otherwise unnecessary wrapper solely to satisfy stale documentation.
- Permission: no README edit made; explicit user permission is required by repository instructions.
- Session-127 source revalidation finds four validateValue mentions in the README and confirms the current named exports include validate but not validateValue. `/tmp/toolcraft-session127-readme-source-audit.log` records this static comparison; prior public-runtime evidence remains separate.

### TC-010: README documents an invalid OneOf call

- Surface: schema API documentation; source: `packages/toolcraft-schema/README.md`, Builders.
- Documented shape: `S.OneOf([schemaA, schemaB], { description?, default? })`.
- Actual shape: `S.OneOf({ discriminator, branches })`, implemented in `packages/toolcraft-schema/src/oneof.ts`.
- Reproduce: call the documented array form with two object schemas from JavaScript.
- Observed: `TypeError: Cannot convert undefined or null to object`.
- Expected: a usable example of the discriminated-union API; JavaScript misuse should ideally receive a descriptive configuration error as a separate developer-experience improvement.
- Remediation: correct the documented signature and provide a small discriminator example. Do not alter the established API to match stale documentation.
- Permission: no README edit made.
- Session-127 source revalidation confirms the README still uses the array/options form while the actual function takes one config object with discriminator and branches. The same source-only AST log, `/tmp/toolcraft-session127-readme-source-audit.log`, records the mismatch without consuming mutable build artifacts during full validation.

### TC-011: nested SDK/MCP casing collisions drop required parameters

- Surface: SDK construction and invocation; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, `validateUniqueSDKParameterMembers` and `validateObjectSchema`.
- Schema: `S.Object({ payload: S.Array(S.Object({ "foo-bar": S.String(), foo_bar: S.String() })) })`.
- SDK input: `{ payload: [{ fooBar: "value" }] }`.
- Observed: SDK construction succeeds and the handler receives `{ payload: [{ foo_bar: "value" }] }`; required `foo-bar` is absent.
- Expected: reject an ambiguous schema during SDK construction, matching collision checks for ordinary object nesting.
- Cause: uniqueness checking recurses only through direct object schemas. Array-contained objects escape inspection, and the normalized-key Map overwrites one required field.
- Remediation: traverse every container that can contain object members, including arrays, records, OneOf, and Union. Verify those additional containers rather than assume the array reproduction proves them all.
- Additional MCP evidence: use an array item with required `"foo-bar": S.String()` followed by optional `foo_bar: S.Optional(S.String())`. Discovery advertises one required `foo_bar` field; `tools/call` with `{ payload: [{ foo_bar: "value" }] }` succeeds and invokes the handler with only `foo_bar`, dropping the required canonical `foo-bar` property.
- MCP controls: ordinary nested objects get an explicit conflicting-field error. If both array fields are required, the underlying JSON Schema checker instead rejects duplicate required strings before invocation. Making the second colliding field optional leaves the published schema structurally valid and exposes the actual data-loss path. No handler execution is claimed for the both-required MCP control.
- MCP source: `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts`, `validateUniqueMCPParameterFields` and `validateObjectSchema`, which duplicate the direct-object-only recursion and normalized-key map behavior.
- Fix: replace the two shallow construction checks with the shared internal `packages/toolcraft/src/schema-member-names.ts` traversal. It checks every declared object through optional/array/record/union/discriminator containers and reserves each discriminator's injected wire name within its own branch. SDK parameters and MCP parameters/results/events use their actual casing rules. Scope projection still happens first for inputs; arbitrary record keys, JSON content, and additional properties are not treated as declared members.
- TDD: the initial construction matrix failed 92 cases with 12 direct-object controls passing. Expanded coverage totals 159 cases in `packages/toolcraft/src/schema-member-collisions.test.ts`: ordinary/stream SDK and MCP params, snake/camel MCP results/events, injected-tag aliases, and valid runtime controls. Alternative union branches and sibling objects keep separate member namespaces; reused branches can appear under different discriminators. Scoped-out aliases do not create false collisions.
- Compatibility boundary: SDK results/events remain canonical and are not case-renamed, so their schemas are intentionally not subjected to this wire-name restriction; two SDK execution controls preserve both authored keys. The new guard validates definitions at construction and does not claim to support arbitrary descriptor mutation after SDK construction. The result/event matrix verifies admission behavior, not historical transport delivery for every invalid schema.
- Verification: all 159 new cases and the Toolcraft package's 72 files / 2,048 tests pass, together with package ESLint/TypeScript. The selected 18-workspace build closure and OpenAPI/codemode consumers (684 and 43 tests) pass. Rebuilt public APIs pass 13 collision/valid-control probes each on Node 18.18.2 and 22.23.2. The maintained root-help screenshot route finishes its 70 uncached builds and bundle; refreshed help and `screenshots/toolcraft-member-collisions.png` were visually inspected. Existing collision diagnostics retain their member names and surface labels.

### TC-012: SDK silently accepts non-plain objects

- Surface: SDK object parameters; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, `isPlainObject` and `validateObjectSchema`.
- Reproduce: `schema = S.Object({})`, `input = new Date(0)`.
- Observed: the SDK invokes the handler with `{ payload: {} }`, silently discarding the Date. Shared validation rejects the input.
- Expected: the object schema's runtime contract is consistent across entrypoints; non-plain values are rejected rather than silently converted.
- Cause: the SDK predicate checks only object/non-null/non-array, despite its name. The shared predicate also checks the prototype.
- Remediation: align object/record acceptance, including null-prototype records, and add explicit tests for Date, Map, and class instances.
- Related diagnostic issue: shared validation currently says `Expected object ... got object` for this case, which does not explain why the value is invalid.
- Session-86 current reproduction: public SDK calls accept a Date object, Map record, class object, and tagged class while shared validation rejects them. A fifth MCP-result probe prints the successful empty-object result but its draft assertion expects the wrong message-session envelope; that fixture error is not a product failure. The actual MCP result is nested under `result` and includes `structuredContent`. Log: `/tmp/toolcraft-session86-nonplain-public-red.log`.
- Local repair under verification: export/reuse the existing schema `isPlainRecord` predicate for SDK inputs, schema-shaped MCP inputs/results, and variant validation. Reject non-plain values before conversion can discard their prototype; shared/adapter diagnostics identify a `non-plain object`. Shared union validation also reports the invalid object rather than masking it as zero matching branches. Preserve the existing missing-own-discriminator diagnostic before checking a supplied tag's object prototype.
- TDD: the first two new files report 120 failures / 27 controls (147 tests). The MCP-result assertions initially expect tool-result errors, but existing result-schema violations use protocol error `-32603`; corrected that fixture without changing protocol behavior. The first green attempt still has those 20 assertion failures and five shared-union diagnostic failures. Expanded tests total 164 cases, adding nullability, omitted optionals, in-memory MCP root inputs, and raw content-block controls.
- Verification corrections: the first package run has six own-discriminator diagnostic regressions plus a bundle failure and a subprocess timeout caused by using the new export before rebuilding schema artifacts. Preserve diagnostic precedence and run the maintained selected build before package tests; no timeout is increased. Corrected session 99161 is terminal, exit 0: 19 builds, seven focused files / 602 tests, 11 schema files / 2,412 tests, 95 Toolcraft files / 4,904 tests, 42 OpenAPI/codemode files / 1,056 tests, and both affected packages' lint/types pass.
- Rebuilt public evidence: 120 checks per exact Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0, and 22.23.2 (360 total), including ordinary/stream SDK inputs, MCP results, shared validation/predicate agreement, and MCP stream terminal/data/finalizer controls. The latter retain shared stream admission rather than claiming streams previously accepted every invalid value. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session86-{nonplain-red,focused-green,focused-corrected-green,focused-expanded,toolcraft,build-corrected,focused-final,schema-final,toolcraft-final,consumers-final,schema-lint-final,toolcraft-lint-final,public-green}.log`.
- Compatibility/visual boundary: ordinary and null-prototype records, nullability, optional omission, explicitly unrestricted opaque extra values, raw MCP content-block structural recognition, and canonical unvalidated SDK return values remain supported. In-memory MCP class-input tests do not claim class instances travel over JSON wire transports. Invalid mutated defaults remain TC-039. Inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-nonplain-values.png`, captured from actual CLI fixtures that invoke SDK calls with JavaScript values; failed calls exit one with zero target-handler executions, and the null-prototype control succeeds. Transcript: `/tmp/toolcraft-session86-visual.log`. Broader verification remains pending; TC-012 is not yet recounted as fixed.
- Session-87 verification is now terminal: normal build, all four maintained packaging/runtime smoke routes, 35,020 shared tests, affected and other recorded native controls, repository/package lint, root help, and maintained types pass. The full unit command still fails its one committed-lock qualification because of session 83's uncommitted lock; this is retained as a failed full gate, not bypassed. Refreshed root help is inspected. TC-012 is locally fixed for supplied typed values and the recorded diagnostics, with that explicit broader qualification boundary. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-audit-session87.MwIFCH`.

### TC-013: bigint invalid input crashes error formatting

- Surface: SDK; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, `describeReceived`.
- Reproduce: `schema = S.Number()`, `input = 1n` from JavaScript or an untyped boundary.
- Observed: `TypeError: Do not know how to serialize a BigInt`, instead of a parameter `UserError`. The handler does not run.
- Expected: report the received bigint type without trying to JSON-serialize it.
- Cause: the error formatter calls `JSON.stringify(value)` on remaining primitive kinds. Formatting also yields undefined for symbols/functions and loses non-finite number identity.
- Local remediation: describe bigint, function, and symbol inputs by type; format numeric primitives without JSON serialization so NaN and infinities retain their identity. Ordinary string/object/array formatting is unchanged.
- Regression evidence: six added cases failed before this change: bigint threw TypeError, functions/symbols were described as undefined, and NaN/infinities were described as null. All six now pass, alongside the 13 JSON cases.
- Relationship to TC-001: its first fix attempt exposed this formatter failure for JSON bigint. The final JSON patch uses the shared validator's received-type description, and this second fix repairs the general SDK diagnostic formatter.

### TC-014: parity harness changes the supplied test scenario

- Surface: developer test tooling; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/testing/parity.ts`, `filterParams`, called by each surface runner.
- Fixture: an all-surface `echo` command with `S.Object({ name: S.String() })` and a handler returning `params`.
- Input: `{ name: "Ada", unexpected: true }`.
- Observed: `harness.run(["echo"], input)` fails at `params`, but `harness.parity(["echo"], input)` returns three successful `{ name: "Ada" }` outcomes with `agree: true`.
- Expected: parity exercises the supplied input, including invalid values, except for intentional surface-scoped exclusions. It must not silently turn invalid input into a different successful test case.
- Cause: `filterParams` rebuilds input from declared schema keys and discards every unknown key before any adapter sees it.
- Impact: tests can report agreement while never checking unknown-argument rejection. Schema-native SDK casing and explicit undefined also need separate investigation because this same filtering stage handles them.
- Remediation: retain unknown keys and supplied values; remove only fields explicitly excluded from the selected scope. Verify rejection on real SDK/MCP/CLI adapters, not solely the direct harness.
- Casing follow-up: commands declaring `user_id`, `user-id`, or nested `config.display_name` work through the real SDK with the corresponding camel-cased input, and through MCP/CLI with their expected translated inputs. Passing canonical input to `parity()` makes only its SDK route fail because it forwards canonical keys without SDK mapping. Passing SDK-shaped input instead makes all three parity routes fail because the declared-key filter drops those keys. In these observed cases `agree` is false, not a false successful pass.
- The direct `harness.run()` uses SDK argument validation and succeeds with SDK-shaped input. An initial probe incorrectly assumed it accepted canonical non-camel names; that assumption was corrected before recording the parity comparisons. The finding is not that the real SDK or direct harness should accept the wrong casing.
- Variant follow-up: a valid `payload` discriminated object succeeds in direct SDK/MCP calls and in a real CLI call using `--payload.kind text --payload.value Ada`. The parity harness instead emits a single `--payload` flag with a serialized object, causing `Unknown option "--payload"` and a false disagreement. Its CLI argument builder handles ordinary objects and records but not variant selectors/branch flags.
- These follow-ups extend the same scenario-fidelity issue: the helper's transformed invocation differs from the valid/invalid input it claims to compare. Establish a consistent harness input convention and schema-aware translation without validating away failures before the actual adapters execute; preserve unknown and explicit-undefined cases rather than masking them.
- Current revalidation on Node 18.18.0 / 20.0.0 / 22.23.2 covers 22 fixtures per runtime: 198 independent real-adapter calls and 198 adapter calls inside 66 parity comparisons. Twelve comparisons falsely accept invalid unknown keys at the root, nested object, optional nested object and grandchild; all independent SDK/MCP/CLI controls reject those inputs. Twenty-four comparisons falsely disagree for canonical/SDK-shaped names, object arrays, object-valued records and oneOf/union branches, although each independent adapter accepts its correctly expressed equivalent. Thirty healthy comparisons cover false booleans, string/number arrays, array-valued records, literal JSON/record keys, ordinary nested objects, required/optional omission and an absent scope-specific field. Inputs remain unchanged. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session129-parity-inputs-18.18.0.log`, `/tmp/toolcraft-session129-parity-inputs-20.0.0.log`, `/tmp/toolcraft-session129-parity-inputs-22.23.2.log`.
- Collection serialization is a separate symptom of the same input-rewriting cause: object arrays become a single `--entries` argument rather than indexed leaf flags, while object-valued records become serialized parent values rather than `--entries.alpha.name`. The genuine CLI accepts the indexed/dotted forms. This does not imply that ordinary CLI object-container presence/defaults (TC-096) are repaired. Inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-parity-inputs-current.png` is derived from `/tmp/toolcraft-session129-parity-inputs-transcript.log` and includes both failures and a healthy control.
- Explicit-undefined follow-up closes the earlier investigation gap. Across the three runtimes, 24 fixtures compare 48 direct SDK/harness calls with 72 parity-adapter calls. Six required-field scenarios with a declared default reject an explicitly owned `undefined` through both the SDK and `harness.run()`, but all parity routes succeed after filtering it into omission and applying the default. Eighteen omitted-field or optional-field controls behave consistently. This proves that parity changes its own SDK scenario; it does not claim that JSON MCP arguments or shell flags can transport JavaScript `undefined` unchanged. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session129-parity-undefined-18.18.0-corrected.log`, `/tmp/toolcraft-session129-parity-undefined-20.0.0-corrected.log`, `/tmp/toolcraft-session129-parity-undefined-22.23.2-corrected.log`.

### TC-015: focused tests require an undisclosed fixture build

- Surface: contributor testing; sources: `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp-proxy-integration.test.ts`, `packages/toolcraft/package.json`, `turbo.json`, `scripts/build-workspaces.mjs`.
- Reproduce in a fresh worktree: install locked dependencies, run `npm run build:workspaces -- --workspace=toolcraft`, then `npm run test:unit --workspace=toolcraft`.
- Observed: six MCP integration failures, including a 5-second timeout. The expected fixture path `packages/tiny-stdio-mcp-test-server/dist/cli.js` did not exist.
- Recovery: `npm run build:workspaces -- --workspace=tiny-stdio-mcp-test-server` made all seven MCP integration cases pass without changing tests or increasing timeouts. The full package suite subsequently passed.
- Expected: a maintained narrow test route declares or clearly exposes its required fixture builds; a missing fixture should produce an actionable setup error rather than an opaque transport exit and timeout.
- Additional graph evidence: calling `createWorkspaceTestPlan(process.cwd())` includes `toolcraft#test:unit` but reports `fixtureBuildSelected: false` for `tiny-stdio-mcp-test-server`, with no test-server build stages. A complete clean-root `npm test` failure was not reproduced and is not claimed here.
- Remediation: express fixture dependencies through maintained task declarations and retain subprocess testing. Do not hide the failure by mocking away the behavior this integration suite exists to exercise.
- Session-79 current-graph check: copied only current root/workspace manifests and Turbo declarations into `memfs`, then called the maintained `createWorkspaceTestPlan`. The baseline still selects `toolcraft#test:unit` without the fixture build. An in-memory candidate adding `tiny-stdio-mcp-test-server` as a Toolcraft development dependency and `toolcraft#test:unit.dependsOn: ["^build"]` selects the fixture after its build dependencies and preserves test-task membership. No repository manifest/lockfile is edited and no test/build process executes in this probe. This validates a declarative direction, not a completed fix or fresh-worktree reproduction. Log: `/tmp/toolcraft-session79-fixture-dependency-analysis.log`.
- Session-83 local fix: reconfirmed the omitted fixture on the current graph, then parsed and deep-merged only the Toolcraft development dependency, its lockfile workspace entry, and the Turbo unit-task dependency declaration. Existing manifest/prepack changes are preserved. The graph gains one dependency edge (209 to 210), preserves all 40 unit tasks, selects the fixture in normal/fresh/excluded-virtual-bash unit plans, and keeps the cached-only group unchanged. No runner implementation, integration-test substitution, timeout, runtime dependency, or bundled dependency is changed.
- Verification: the maintained selected Toolcraft build executes the fixture and its dependencies (19 builds); all seven actual MCP subprocess integration cases and all 94 Toolcraft files / 4,760 tests pass. Toolcraft lint/types, all 17 package-lint rules, and whitespace checks pass. The npm-owned graph suites pass 196 tests. An initial direct-Vitest invocation lacks the required invoking npm CLI path and fails ten lifecycle controls; the corrected invocation is `npm run test:unit -- scripts/build-workspaces.test.ts scripts/build-workspaces.parallel.test.ts`, not a production workaround.
- Lock verification: copied only the current root/71 workspace manifests and lockfile into an isolated temporary directory, ran offline `npm install --package-lock-only --ignore-scripts --no-audit --no-fund`, and verified zero semantic lock changes and an unchanged repository lock. No installation/build scripts execute in that check. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session83-{build,integration,package,lint,package-lint,diff,task-graph,graph-tests,graph-tests-npm,lock-validation}.log`. This is graph, real build/subprocess, and isolated lock evidence, not another clean-worktree install claim. A bare workspace `test:unit` command still expects the documented build preparation; the maintained root unit runner now declares it automatically.
- Full-gate boundary: snapshot 84's shared tests pass, but its native committed-export qualification rejects the new uncommitted lockfile against `HEAD`. The only semantic lock difference is this fixture declaration. The full run fails; it is not a successful post-fix snapshot. Preserve the valid lock change and committed-artifact check, and do not create an unauthorized commit or substitute a revision/profile to manufacture a pass. See session 84 for exact counts and the remaining-check run.

### TC-016: screenshot preparation cannot bootstrap its own dependency

- Surface: contributor visual QA; sources: `scripts/screenshot.ts`, root `package.json`.
- Reproduce after a Toolcraft-only build in a fresh worktree: `npm run screenshot-poe-code -- --help`.
- Observed: immediate `ERR_MODULE_NOT_FOUND` for `terminal-png/dist/index.js`; no screenshot or preparation step runs.
- Cause: the script statically imports `terminal-png` before it can execute the `predev` preparation declared by `resolveScreenshotTarget`.
- Recovery: build `terminal-png` using `npm run build:workspaces -- --workspace=terminal-png`, then retry the same screenshot command. The retry builds the repository, bundles the CLI, and produces `screenshots/help.png`.
- Expected: the advertised screenshot command either bootstraps its dependency before loading it or reports the prerequisite with a clear recovery command.
- Scope: this is a fresh-worktree developer-experience issue, not a failure in a fully built checkout. No defect in the screenshot image renderer is inferred.
- Remediation: remove the eager renderer import. Each transcript/PTY capture path loads `terminal-png` after declared preparation succeeds, but before starting the captured command or terminal pilot. The existing maintained build route and dependency graph remain unchanged.
- Session-68 TDD: the corrected in-memory regression fixture reports nine failures and two existing-renderer controls before the patch; all 11 cases pass afterward. Both capture modes cover preparation failure, successful bootstrap, missing renderer after preparation, generic commands, and importable target utilities. The old timeout test now emits output after mocked child creation rather than before asynchronous renderer loading can spawn the child. Two focused files / 29 tests, focused ESLint, and root types pass.
- Final verification: full snapshot 69 passes normal build, packaging smoke routes, every maintained test stage, and repository lint. Inspected `screenshots/help.png` and `screenshots/toolcraft-bootstrap-pty.png` after maintained transcript/forced-PTY captures. The initial 60-row PTY viewport cropped the beginning of long help; recapturing at 120 rows includes the full output. The final PTY preparation is forced uncached and all 70 builds run, followed by root types. Status: locally fixed. The missing-renderer transition is covered by the in-memory regression; the final actual captures use the current checkout, not a newly created fresh-worktree end-to-end run.

### TC-017: debug logging exposes query credentials

- Surface: OpenAPI HTTP diagnostics, including CLI verbose output; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/http.ts`, `requestJson` and `fetchWithRetries`.
- Reproduce with a fake fetch: request `/items?api_key=AUDIT_QUERY_SECRET` with `createRuntimeLogger({ level: "trace", logger })` and inspect every event, not only transcript fields.
- Observed: the initial debug event contains the real credential in both `message` and `data.url`. The trace transcript masks it, but trace level also includes the leaking debug event. A 503 retry repeats the raw URL; network-error retries do too.
- Expected: query credentials recognized by the existing redaction policy stay out of every diagnostic URL, while the actual HTTP request retains its real credential.
- Cause: transcript/error construction uses `redactSensitiveQueryValues`; debug request and retry messages used the original URL.
- Local remediation: reuse the existing URL redactor for the initial debug event and both retry paths. The request URL itself is not changed.
- TDD evidence: four cases failed before the patch: initial debug logging, initial trace-level logging, status retry, and network retry. All four pass with adjacent HTTP/network tests (83 focused tests total).
- Scope: this fixes framework-generated URL fields for recognized query keys. It does not claim to sanitize arbitrary custom exception messages, URL userinfo, unknown credential names, headers, or bodies; TC-018 and TC-019 remain separate open findings.

### TC-018: API-key headers bypass transcript/error redaction

- Surfaces: request/response trace transcripts and `HttpError` request/response headers; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/redaction.ts`, `redactHeaderValue`.
- Reproduce: set request header `X-Api-Key: AUDIT_HEADER_SECRET`; return `x-api-key: AUDIT_RESPONSE_HEADER_SECRET` from a fake fetch. Capture trace diagnostics for a successful response and structured errors for a 400 response.
- Observed: both exact synthetic secret values appear in the transcript and in the public error header objects. Bearer Authorization and cookies are masked by the same machinery.
- Expected: commonly named API-key credentials receive consistent protection, case-insensitively, without altering headers sent to the API.
- Cause: the OpenAPI helper recognizes Authorization plus a small cookie/proxy-header allowlist, but does not recognize API-key headers. Toolcraft's separate core HTTP-header redactor does recognize secret-like names, creating inconsistent behavior.
- Impact: users enabling verbose output or serializing structured errors can disclose credentials to log consumers.
- Remediation: converge on a shared documented header-redaction policy and test request/response headers at mixed casing. Verify that wire headers retain original values and that public non-sensitive headers remain useful.
- Local fix: OpenAPI now uses core `isSensitiveName` alongside its existing cookie/Authorization handling. Request and response `X-Api-Key`, `x-AUTH-token`, and `Client_Secret` variants are masked in trace and structured error headers. Two status-parameterized regressions cover success and failure without changing wire values.
- Boundary: all reproduction values are synthetic. No production credentials or remote services were used.

### TC-019: trace bodies expose passwords and access tokens

- Surface: HTTP trace transcripts; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/http.ts`, `formatTranscriptBody`.
- Reproduce: POST `{ password: "AUDIT_BODY_SECRET", name: "visible" }` through a fake fetch returning `{ access_token: "AUDIT_RESPONSE_SECRET", ok: true }`, with trace diagnostics enabled.
- Observed: both credentials appear verbatim in the captured transcript; truncation only limits length and does not mask them.
- Expected: secret-like structured fields are masked in diagnostics while request and return values remain intact for application logic.
- Cause: `formatTranscriptBody` stringifies structured bodies directly. Core Toolcraft has a separate `redactHttpBody` implementation, but this transcript path does not use an equivalent policy.
- Remediation: redact structured request and response bodies before transcript formatting/truncation, including arrays and JSON-encoded strings. Define separate handling for forms and opaque raw/binary content; do not silently change actual request bodies or returned data.
- Local fix: transcript formatting serializes non-string input first, applies core `redactHttpBody` to its JSON representation, then formats/truncates the redacted value. This preserves JSON serialization of Dates and redacts values produced by `toJSON`, rather than copying callable serialization hooks into an allegedly redacted object.
- Regression coverage: JSON/form/raw-JSON request bodies, nested credentials, structured and JSON-encoded text responses, Date formatting, and custom JSON serialization. The request payload and returned response retain their original values.
- Shared API: `toolcraft` now exports `isSensitiveName(name)` and `redactHttpBody(body)` for reuse by the OpenAPI package. The former recognizes normalized password/token/apikey/secret names; the latter masks structured fields and JSON object/array strings. It is not an arbitrary-text credential detector.
- Boundary: opaque text, binary payloads, and credentials under unrecognized field names are not claimed safe. The separate core report serialization path is covered by the later TC-031 fix.
- Scope: this finding concerns emitted diagnostics. It does not assert that an API returning raw response data to its caller is itself a defect.

### TC-020: cancellation is treated as a retryable network failure

- Surface: OpenAPI HTTP retry handling; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/http.ts`, `fetchWithRetries` and `sleepBeforeRetry`.
- Reproduce: pass an already-aborted signal, configure `max: 2`, and inject a fake fetch that invokes `init.signal.throwIfAborted()` plus a fake sleep counter.
- Observed: three fetch invocations and two sleep invocations occur before a `UserError` saying the request was aborted. No network request was made by this reproduction.
- Expected: an explicitly cancelled operation terminates without retry/backoff work. Cancellation during a backoff should also be responsive.
- Cause: the catch block classifies AbortError but still retries it; the loop has no cancellation guard, and retry sleep does not receive the signal.
- Remediation: distinguish cancellation from retryable failures, check cancellation at attempt boundaries, and make backoff interruption deterministic. Test pre-abort, in-flight abort, and abort during a pending injected sleep with fake timers/deferred promises rather than real waits.
- Local fix: attempt boundaries reject cancelled signals before fetch; fetch AbortErrors, including wrapped causes, do not retry. Custom signal reasons retain their cause in a redacted-URL UserError. Backoff listens for cancellation, clears its native timer, and removes its listener on success, failure, or abort. A cancelled discarded-response cleanup cannot emit a misleading retry message or start a sleep.
- TDD: all ten initial cancellation reproductions failed before implementation. A follow-up retry-log assertion failed before adding the post-cleanup guard; 14 controls passed. The final 15 cases cover pre-abort reasons, direct/wrapped AbortErrors, in-flight custom cancellation, query redaction, response cleanup, network/status backoff, timer clearing, listener cleanup, synchronous/asynchronous sleep failures, and late sleep rejection after cancellation.
- Verification: 26 OpenAPI files / 533 tests pass, plus focused ESLint and package TypeScript checks. Fake timers/deferred promises avoid real backoff waits. `screenshots/toolcraft-http-cancellation.png` captures inspected actual CLI errors for pre-aborted and cancelled-backoff calls, with fetch/sleep counters and credential-redaction assertions; no real network request was made.
- Boundary: the injected `sleep(ms)` signature remains compatible. Toolcraft stops waiting for an injected sleep, but cannot cancel arbitrary side effects owned by that callback. This change does not make a hanging custom fetch, token source, response-body cleanup, or response parser forcibly interruptible. Normal native fetch still receives the caller's signal. A later consolidated full verification was started, not yet claimed successful here.
- Related finding: the broad catch also misclassified logging/sleep failures. That behavior is independently reproduced and fixed as TC-034; the separate cancellation fix is documented here.

### TC-021: dot-only path parameters change the target route

- Surface: HTTP request URL construction; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/http.ts`, `substitutePathParams` and `buildRequestUrl`.
- Reproduce with fake fetch capture: `baseUrl: "https://api.example.test"`, `path: "/items/{id}"`, `pathParams: { id: ".." }`.
- Observed URL passed to fetch: `https://api.example.test/`, not an item route. The operation succeeds against the fake root response without reporting an invalid identifier.
- Expected: either preserve the requested identifier as data or reject unsupported dot-segment identifiers before issuing a request; never silently select another route.
- Cause: `encodeURIComponent("..")` leaves the value as dots, and assigning the combined path to `URL.pathname` normalizes that segment away.
- Impact: a generated operation can call a different endpoint under the configured base URL. Only a harmless fake GET was used; no destructive request or exploit is claimed.
- Remediation: validate path-parameter segment behavior before transport. Cover `.` and `..`, base-path prefixes, identifiers containing encoded slashes, and ordinary punctuation without relying on blanket double-encoding.
- Extended current-source evidence: a 14-case fake-fetch probe confirms removal of dot segments at a base-path prefix, in the middle of an operation path, from two adjacent `"."` parameters, from parameter/template combinations such as `%2e{id}`, and after a literal backslash separator. Safe controls retain `..` inside `{id}.json` or `prefix{id}`, preserve encoded-slash identifiers, double-encode a caller's literal `%2e` as data, and retain `...`.
- Fix-design constraint: rejecting every value equal to `.`/`..` would unnecessarily reject the safe embedded controls while still missing combined/encoded template segments. Compare URL-normalized path structure against the same template filled with ordinary placeholder data, or implement an equivalent complete segment check. Preserve intentionally static dot segments and do not echo potentially sensitive parameter values in the rejection.
- Fix: compare native-URL-normalized path structure with a neutral-filled template before dispatch. Both comparison paths receive a temporary trailing segment so a terminal `.` cannot disappear into an indistinguishable trailing slash. The comparison URL never reaches fetch; the actual request retains its original encoding, query, fragment, and ordinary static-path normalization. Reject route-changing substitutions with a `UserError` that does not expose actual parameter values.
- TDD: 72 unsafe cases failed before implementation and 79 compatibility controls passed. All 151 cases now pass in `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/path-parameters.test.ts`; the full OpenAPI package passes 27 files / 684 cases plus ESLint and TypeScript. The matrix crosses GET/POST and three HTTP(S)/base-prefix forms, covers literal/encoded/combined dot segments, backslashes and template control characters, and preserves safe embedded dots, empty values, booleans, numbers, encoded slashes, base query/fragment, and one-time accessor evaluation.
- Boundaries: the guarantee concerns the URL passed to transport, not arbitrary additional decoding by an external server. Authentication/body preparation precedes URL construction in the existing request path; the fix prevents fetch and idempotency-key generation for the tested invalid paths, not every possible earlier caller callback. The tests use `auth: "none"` and fake fetch, with no remote destructive request.
- Rebuilt integration evidence: an actual `defineClientFromSpec` operation is exercised through SDK, CLI, and MCP on Node 18.18.2 and 22.23.2. Each runtime passes 15 scenarios: dot/dot-dot rejection with zero transport calls, and ordinary/slash-containing/literal-percent identifiers retaining exact expected URLs. MCP failures keep `-32602`; CLI failures exit one. The inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-path-parameters.png` also shows that a safe embedded `..` in `{id}.json` remains usable.

### TC-022: client composition removes command execution contracts

- Surface: `defineClient` composition of generated/handwritten commands; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/define-client.ts` and shared `cloneCommandNode` in `packages/toolcraft/src/index.ts`. Earlier reproductions concern the replaced private `cloneCommand` / `cloneGroup` implementation.
- Approval reproduction: define `deploy` with sync `humanInLoop`, a synthetic handler, and no wired runtime. Direct `createSDK` rejects configuration. After `defineClient`, the command's approval config becomes undefined and the SDK executes the handler successfully.
- Stream reproduction: compose a `defineStreamCommand`. Calling its SDK member returns a Promise with no async iterator or `cancel`, rather than the managed stream returned by direct Toolcraft construction.
- Metadata reproduction: compose a command declaring `title`, `annotations`, `result`, and `mcpResult`. The resulting MCP tool loses its title, annotations, and output schema; its result projection is also dropped.
- Expected: composing a client preserves command safety requirements, streaming semantics, and declared MCP contracts. Adding a handwritten child to a protected group must retain inherited approval requirements.
- Cause: cloning enumerated a subset of older command/group fields and always reconstructed commands with `defineCommand`, including stream commands.
- Local remediation: preserve public command/group properties while retaining the existing defensive copies; use `defineStreamCommand` for stream nodes. Explicit type arguments preserve the generic service and scope contract without unsafe runtime casts.
- TDD evidence: four cases failed before the change. They now verify startup wiring checks plus declined-approval handler suppression, protected-group inheritance after merging, lazy stream iteration/cancellation, and MCP metadata/output projection over an in-memory client/server pair. Adjacent composition tests also pass (17 tests total).
- Historical boundary: no real deployment or external approval was attempted. Proxy-group configuration and internal/source-location metadata were not covered by the initial fix. Session 113 confirms both as remaining defects and reopens this finding; the earlier repairs are preserved.
- Proxy reproduction: configure a group with a local stdio MCP upstream, an `echo` allowlist, and rename `echo` to `remote.echo`. Compare an original Toolcraft root against `defineClient` composition constructed before discovery. Generated-only, handwritten-only, nested, proxy-before-manual, and manual-before-proxy compositions all lose `hasMcpProxyConfig`, expose no `remote.echo` SDK member, and omit that tool from MCP discovery. Original roots discover and invoke the renamed typed tool successfully. The composed roots retain their ordinary manual commands. Initial evidence: `/tmp/toolcraft-session113-client-proxy-before.log`.
- MCP/CLI effect: original MCP calls return `{ id: "echo" }`; composed calls to the same logical path fail as unknown tools. Actual original group help lists `remote` / `echo`, while composed group help silently lists neither and still exits zero. An ordinary generated/handwritten merge remains healthy. This is definition loss, not an upstream connection failure or an allowlist/rename rejection. Inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-client-proxy-before.png` records the actual CLI contrast.
- Fixture-source reproduction: define an ordinary command from an in-memory program with a real VM source filename in an owned temporary directory, and write only its adjacent JSON fixture. Original `TOOLCRAFT_FIXTURE=sample` CLI calls load that fixture and return `{ value: 42 }`. After composition, `getCommandSourcePath` points to Toolcraft OpenAPI's `define-client.js`, and both top-level and nested CLI calls instead search for `define-client.fixture.json` beside the framework module and exit 1. The command source is evaluated in memory; no executable QA file is saved. Live service callbacks remain uncalled. Evidence: `/tmp/toolcraft-session113-client-fixture-before-corrected.log` and inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-client-fixture-before.png`.
- Fixture correction: the first draft incorrectly accesses `context.services.counter` rather than the actual top-level `context.counter`, so even the original control fails. That is a probe mistake, retained in `/tmp/toolcraft-session113-client-fixture-before.log` and `/tmp/toolcraft-session113-client-fixture-diagnostic.log`. Only the corrected control/contrast is defect evidence; no production handler-context change is proposed.
- Session 113 causes: group reconstruction spreads public enumerable fields, while `mcp`, `tools`, and `rename` remain in a non-enumerable definition symbol. Command reconstruction reruns `defineCommand`/`defineStreamCommand`, recapturing the framework call site rather than retaining the original source location. In addition, same-path group merging currently merges children only, so preserving a standalone proxy definition alone would not establish correct proxy/manual composition in both orders.
- Exact-runtime evidence: Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0, and 22.23.2 each reproduce all five proxy losses with a healthy ordinary merge, including real SDK discovery/invocation controls and twelve initialized MCP calls. Total: eighteen proxy/control scenarios, 36 MCP calls with fifteen expected missing-tool errors, and twelve actual fixture-mode CLI calls with six known failures and six healthy controls. Handler/live-call counts and expected outputs/errors are asserted; source paths and command paths are recorded. Evidence: `/tmp/toolcraft-session113-composition-native-before.log`. Temporary data, environment overrides, and owned upstream processes are cleaned up.
- Existing regression coverage is insufficient for these paths: the maintained `define-client.test.ts` and `client-composition.test.ts` suites still pass all seventeen tests. Log: `/tmp/toolcraft-session113-existing-composition-tests.log`. Those passing controls preserve earlier behavior; they are not evidence that opaque metadata survives composition.
- Session 113 repair requirements: use one definition-preserving reconstruction contract rather than making OpenAPI inspect or mutate private symbols. Retain original source paths, proxy transport/filter/rename settings, effective command metadata, default-child references, immutable inputs, and merged descendants after reparenting. No source fix was claimed in that session.
- Further baseline: inherited checks are copied as already-resolved parent functions and applied again. At nesting depths zero through three, original calls run 1 / 2 / 3 / 4 preflights, while composed calls run 1 / 4 / 10 / 20. A stateful local check that permits its first call and rejects a repeat incorrectly prevents the composed handler. Separately, composing an already-resolved proxy retains generated handler closures but loses the owning proxy definition, so both trees invoke the same upstream process. These extend the same copy-boundary finding rather than adding duplicate inventory. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session114-preflight-public-before.log`, `/tmp/toolcraft-session114-hot-copy-before.log`.
- Local repair: exported `cloneCommandNode` reconstructs effective root metadata and intrinsic descendant definitions once, retaining original command source paths and proxy configuration. It preserves current manual descendants/default references, excludes runtime-derived proxy children only within proxy ownership, and does not copy live connections. OpenAPI uses this operation instead of private metadata inspection. A small shared proxy-node marker module keeps the core copy operation independent of the MCP runtime. Functions and schemas remain shared by the existing builder contract; this is not arbitrary application-state cloning. `ClonedCommandNode` retains ordinary inference without claiming custom runtime properties; a scope override intentionally drops the old static scope annotation. See `docs/toolcraft-composition.md`.
- TDD: initial 27-case run has 26 failures and one control. After source repair, an extra standalone proxy-handler control brings the new cases to 28; all five focused suites pass 107 tests. A compile-check first fails because returning exact `TNode` falsely promises an extra runtime property, then passes with the conditional definition return type. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session114-definition-red.log`, `/tmp/toolcraft-session114-definition-final.log`, `/tmp/toolcraft-session114-types-red.log`, `/tmp/toolcraft-session114-types-green.log`.
- Exact-runtime repair verification: Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0, and 22.23.2 each pass original/composed depth and stateful SDK checks, twelve actual fixture CLI calls total, and six proxy/control scenarios per runtime. Of 36 proxy MCP calls, 33 succeed and three deliberately reproduce the remaining manual-before-proxy missing-tool defect. Four repaired proxy arrangements retain discovery/allowlist/rename behavior; ordinary merges and manual children remain healthy. Six hot-copy scenarios establish separate child PIDs and independent disposal while the original SDK stays usable. An additional twelve CLI and twelve MCP preflight calls confirm once-only checks and stateful success. Logs use `/tmp/toolcraft-session114-{preflight,fixture,proxy,hot-copy}-<version>-after.log` and `/tmp/toolcraft-session114-preflight-surfaces-<version>-corrected.log`.
- Visual verification: inspected actual `screenshots/toolcraft-client-proxy-after.png`, `screenshots/toolcraft-client-fixture-after.png`, and `screenshots/toolcraft-client-preflights-after.png`; proxy help now lists the renamed command and both fixture forms return 42. Native rendering uses the compatible local Node runtime; the in-app Node host rejects the native renderer signature, not the generated output.
- Maintained focused verification: selected OpenAPI build closure succeeds (20 builds, 71 declared workspaces, 210 edges, eleven layers); Toolcraft passes 102 files / 6,838 tests and OpenAPI passes 36 files / 1,280 tests. Toolcraft lint/types pass. OpenAPI has no `lint` script, so the attempted workspace command exits one; direct configured ESLint and package TypeScript checks then pass. The first surface probe omitted CLI control registration and fails before execution on unknown `--yes`; corrected controls pass, with original diagnostics retained.
