No product behavior is changed for either validation-invocation mistake.
- Remaining boundary: same-path group merging still copies children only. An incoming proxy after an ordinary generated group loses its upstream configuration. This exact defect remains reproduced across all three runtimes. A merge policy for two distinct upstream definitions and conflicting group metadata is not established. Status: partially fixed, open for manual-before-proxy merging; standalone/nested/proxy-first copies, fixture paths, repeated preflights, and owned live-copy behavior are locally repaired.

### TC-023: retry responses leave their bodies unsettled

- Surface: HTTP retry lifecycle; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/http.ts`, `fetchWithRetries`.
- Reproduce: return a synthetic 503 `Response` backed by a `ReadableStream` with a cancellation callback; let the next fake fetch return successful JSON.
- Observed after the request finishes: two fetch calls, first response `bodyUsed: false`, `body.locked: false`, and zero cancellation callbacks. The reproduction explicitly cancels the stream afterward for cleanup.
- Expected: release a response that will not be returned or consumed before starting another attempt.
- Cause: the retry path sleeps and loops without consuming or cancelling the discarded response body.
- Impact established here: the discarded stream remains untouched after success. Socket exhaustion or production memory leakage has not been measured and is not claimed.
- Remediation: cancel discarded response bodies at the retry boundary and test cleanup for status retries, cancellation, and sleep failures, including a response with no body. Do not consume arbitrarily large error bodies merely to retry.
- Local fix: cancel a discarded response before retry diagnostics or sleep. Leave final/non-retried responses available for normal parsing. Cleanup failures propagate instead of initiating more attempts.
- Regression evidence: `http-retries.test.ts` checks cancellation order, no-body responses, final-error parsing, cleanup before sleep/logger failures, and rejection from cancellation itself. The initial run had four failures and two controls passing; all six now pass. TC-020 separately covers the subsequently fixed cancellation/backoff responsiveness; no production socket measurement is claimed.

### TC-024: module collisions silently replace host commands

- Surface: codemode host-module construction; source: `packages/toolcraft-codemode/src/host-modules.ts`, `getModuleName` and `buildHostModules`.
- Reproduce: define root `tools` with a top-level `read` command and subgroup `tools` with another `read` command. Their SDK paths are distinct and command-tree discovery lists both `read` and `tools.read`.
- Observed: both are exported as `read` from module `tools`. `lintModules.tools` contains `["read", "read"]`; calling `modules.tools.read({})` executes the nested handler because it overwrites the top-level one. Reversing declaration order changes the winner.
- Expected: detect an ambiguous module/export binding before script execution, with both command paths in the diagnostic. Do not silently choose a handler based on traversal order.
- Cause: tree-path uniqueness is checked, but module export uniqueness was not. Root commands use the root name as module name while nested commands use their group path, allowing distinct tree paths to converge.
- Local remediation: track module/export ownership during host-module construction and throw an actionable `UserError` on collision.
- TDD evidence: two declaration-order cases failed before the change and now pass. Both assert neither handler runs, and the error names the colliding module/export and nested path.
- Scope: invalid trees are rejected when host modules are built. This does not rename modules or claim that catalog construction itself now fails synchronously.

### TC-025: discovery schemas disagree with executable host parameters

- Surfaces: `get_schemas`, detailed/full `search`, and `execute`; sources: `packages/toolcraft-codemode/src/get-schemas.ts`, `search.ts`, and `host-modules.ts`.
- Casing reproduction: a `get-user` command declares `user_id: S.String()`. Discovery advertises required `user_id`. A script importing `get_user` and passing `{ user_id: "one" }` fails with both `Unexpected parameter "user_id"` and `Missing required parameter "userId"`; `{ userId: "one" }` succeeds.
- Scope reproduction: declare `target: S.Optional(S.String({ requiredScopes: ["sdk"] }))` plus `cliOnly: S.Optional(S.String({ scope: ["cli"] }))`. Discovery advertises both fields as optional. Omitting `target` fails as missing; supplying `cliOnly` fails as unexpected.
- Expected: advertised schemas describe the actual SDK-backed host-call interface: SDK casing, scope exclusions, and scope-specific requirements must agree with execution.
- Cause: discovery serializes the original command schema, while execution invokes an SDK adapter that changes field names and scope requirements.
- Impact: an agent following the supplied schema can produce invalid calls even though it correctly uses the catalog. Failure recovery requires guessing a different interface.
- Remediation: derive discovery from the same schema transformation used by the callable surface; avoid a second partial normalization algorithm. Cover nested objects, arrays, records, discriminators, unions, defaults, and scoped requirements before claiming full parity.
- Boundary: the successful SDK-cased call returns schema-native keys from the handler as expected. The defect is input discovery, not the handler's result casing.
- Session-96 current-runtime extension: 12 public catalog/execution scenarios reconfirm casing and scope mismatches and establish default-requiredness mismatches for required scalar defaults, nested object defaults, array-item defaults, and union branches made viable by defaults. A supplied `{ payload: {} }` can execute successfully through a union while the advertised schema rejects it. Record entry keys remain literal while known child member keys are camel-cased. Discriminator fields and branch fields need the same input casing as other known properties. `getSchemas`, detailed search, and full search agree with each other, but all expose the same raw rather than executable schema. `/tmp/toolcraft-session96-codemode-schema-public.log` records the actual schema-compiler decisions, execute results, and handler counts. Nullable string/object and ordinary numeric controls agree with execution; nullable metadata is not promoted as a defect merely because a source search did not locate its evaluator support.
- Separate discovery-ownership defect: the same probe shows returned defaults can mutate later execution. That is independently repaired under TC-098; copying metadata does not fix this finding's casing, scope, or requiredness. TC-025 remains open rather than receiving a partial-parity claim.
- Session-98 preservation requirement: SDK input projection must also describe TC-099's repaired canonical-alias ownership rule. Optional caller fields without defaults cannot be supplied indirectly by an additional key with the canonical name; explicit/defaulted values retain precedence. MCP input discovery now carries matching conditions, but codemode still uses raw schema serialization and is not claimed repaired by that independent fix.
- Session-126 current-state revalidation: 39 scenarios per exact Node 18.18.0 / 20.0.0 / 22.23.2 exercise getSchemas, detailed search, full search and actual execute calls. The 351 discovery calls and 117 executions yield 78 mismatching scenarios and 39 controls: each runtime has eleven advertised-valid/execution-invalid cases and fifteen executable/schema-rejected cases. Source schemas stay unchanged and handler calls match execution decisions. This extends the evidence to optional alias rules, default precedence and null-only exhausted variants while retaining literal JSON/record keys, nullable and exact-one controls. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session126-codemode-projection-<version>.log`.
- The same matrix after TC-115's independent stack-presentation repair retains every mismatch/control decision: `/tmp/toolcraft-session126-codemode-projection-<version>-after.log`. Inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-codemode-projection-current.png` shows actual compiler/execution results. A non-README guide now explicitly describes the calling-convention mismatch; documentation and cleaner diagnostics do not close TC-025.

### TC-026: compound command names are poor search terms

- Surface: codemode command search; source: `packages/toolcraft-codemode/src/search.ts`, `isWordCharacter` and `tokenize`.
- Reproduce: define `get-user` with no description. Its normalized catalog name is `get_user`. Searching `user` returns `[]`; searching `get_user` finds the command.
- Observed cause: underscore is considered part of a word, so normalization joins name components into a single search token. A description containing `user` can conceal this limitation in typical fixtures.
- User impact: a caller who knows the resource noun but not the exact normalized command name may fail to discover an available operation.
- Classification: confirmed usability limitation, not a proven violation of a documented substring-search guarantee. Existing search is token-based and no stemming/fuzzy-search promise is assumed.
- Improvement direction: index normalized identifier components alongside whole names while preserving exact-name ranking. Session 82 implements and verifies this after public revalidation and failing regression tests.
- Session-60 read-only follow-up: nine cases execute the current search-handler functions extracted from source, with an identity command-builder fixture and unused brief-mode schema-serializer stub. Besides reconfirming the compound-name/description controls, exact Chinese, Cyrillic, and Greek names produce no matches because tokenization drops every character. `café` and `cafè` both match the same `café` entry after their accented endings are removed. This is tokenizer/handler evidence, not yet a public command-tree/sandbox Unicode check; no Unicode/fuzzy-search promise is inferred. No workspace dist is read during the full-check freeze. Log: `/tmp/toolcraft-session60-search-source.log`.
- Session-79 read-only design check: extracted the current tokenizer and BM25 functions directly from source during the full-build freeze. A candidate using native word segmentation, existing identifier normalization, retained whole identifiers, and additional identifier-component tokens passes 21 token cases and 14 ranking controls on each exact Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0, and 22.23.2. It distinguishes `café`/`cafè`, treats decomposed/precomposed accents consistently, and retains ASCII punctuation behavior. A first simpler candidate passed 24 tokenizer cases per runtime but did not address raw camelCase queries; the second explicitly covers `getUser` and acronym names. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session79-search-{tokenizer,ranking}-analysis.log`.
- Historical candidate boundary: session 79's probes did not edit production search or establish public command-tree, SDK/MCP, result-limit, or sandbox behavior. They guided the subsequent regression matrix. At that point TC-026 remained open; no stemming, fuzzy matching, transliteration, or exhaustive language-support promise was added.
- Session-82 public revalidation: ten queries reproduce missing compound-name/camelCase/Unicode matches and accent conflation through both SDK and MCP, without executing catalog handlers. The first MCP probe incorrectly decoded only one content block as the entire array; corrected to decode every result block before asserting adapter agreement. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session82-search-public-{red,confirmed}.log`.
- Fix/verification: the tokenizer now uses NFC normalization and native word segmentation, preserving ASCII/apostrophe punctuation behavior and adding existing identifier-normalizer forms/components. The BM25 formula, tie-breaking, result shapes, and limit/detail contracts remain unchanged; richer indexed terms intentionally change relevant matches/ranking. The 60-case regression starts with 42 failures / 18 controls; three focused files / 76 tests and all nine codemode files / 130 tests pass, alongside package lint/types and the selected 29-workspace build. All 86 rebuilt public checks per exact Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0, and 22.23.2 pass (258 total), including SDK/MCP/opted-in CLI, result limits/details, zero handler calls, and decomposed catalog spellings.
- Visual boundary: the actual CLI transcript returns the correct Chinese spelling, but the default screenshot renderer lacks its glyphs (TC-095). Inspected the original `screenshots/toolcraft-search-identifiers.png` and a clearly labeled ad hoc local-font reference, `screenshots/toolcraft-search-identifiers-cjk-reference.png`. This is not a fix to the renderer. Maintained uncached root-help preparation and root types pass; refreshed root help is inspected.
- Status: locally fixed for the confirmed identifier/tokenization issues. No fuzzy matching, transliteration, stemming, exhaustive language coverage, or Unicode sandbox-execution guarantee is added. TC-025's detailed-schema agreement remains separate. Full snapshot 79 predates this patch.

### TC-027: advertised streaming commands cannot execute through codemode

- Surface: codemode discovery and SafeJS host calls; sources: `packages/toolcraft-codemode/src/tree.ts`, `host-modules.ts`, and the SDK managed-stream return contract.
- Reproduce: expose a `defineStreamCommand({ name: "watch", event: S.Number(), ... })` under root `events`. `getSchemas({ names: ["watch"] })` succeeds. Execute `import { watch } from "events"; return await watch({});`.
- Observed: execution returns `ok: false`, `kind: "runtime"`, with `Unsupported sandbox value at <root>.signal: AbortSignal`.
- Expected: discovery should communicate a usable stream calling/consumption contract, or reject unsupported composition explicitly before advertising an ordinary callable command.
- Cause: the host wrapper forwards the SDK's managed-stream object directly into the sandbox bridge; it is not ordinary serializable result data.
- Remediation: design an explicit bounded stream adapter with cancellation and a discoverable result shape, or document and validate the limitation. Silently hiding streams is not equivalent to supporting them.
- Boundary: this reproduces the boundary failure, not lost remote events or an actual resource leak. A session-33 rebuilt public-API probe reconfirms discovery/execution failure and records zero producer starts and zero producer finalizers: the lazy managed stream is rejected by the sandbox before producer iteration. No running-producer leak is demonstrated for this path; this does not establish cleanup for a future stream adapter or every custom host implementation.
- Session-112 current-build revalidation: ordinary and SDK-excluded-stream controls execute successfully; direct and nested SDK-visible streams are returned by tree resolution, search, and schema discovery but fail at execution with the same opaque `AbortSignal` boundary message. Sixteen public checks retain zero producer starts/finalizers. Evidence: `/tmp/toolcraft-session112-stream-public-before.log`; actual CLI baseline `/tmp/toolcraft-session112-stream-cli-before.log` and inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-codemode-stream-before.png`.
- Local repair validates every resolved entry list, including caller-supplied arrays/promises, and rejects an SDK-visible stream catalog with `UserError` naming the normalized command path and suggesting a bounded ordinary command. Direct tree resolution, search, schemas, host-module construction, and execution share this check. No stream is silently advertised or silently removed. Scope-excluded streams remain absent from the default SDK-backed catalog, and ordinary commands in those valid catalogs continue to work.
- Deliberate limitation: this is an explicit unsupported-composition contract, not a streaming adapter. An invalid mixed catalog rejects even an ordinary script that would not call its stream. Users can provide a separate ordinary-command root or a bounded collector with deliberate event/time limits and cleanup. Validation does not start a producer or attempt cancellation. Non-README guidance is in `docs/toolcraft-codemode.md`; TC-025's input-schema projection mismatch remains open.
- TDD: 27 new cases produce 21 failures / six passing scope-exclusion controls before source changes, then pass. Cases include direct/nested and inherited/explicit/all-surface scopes, all three codemode operations, supplied raw/promised entries, host-module construction, ordinary execution, and deferred rejection ownership. The focused three-file run passes 39 tests, and the complete codemode suite passes 11 files / 187 tests. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session112-stream-{red,green}.log` and `/tmp/toolcraft-session112-package.log`.
- Exact-runtime verification: Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0, and 22.23.2 each pass sixteen tree/SDK checks, two actual CLI cases, and six initialized MCP calls: 48 tree/SDK checks, six CLI cases, and eighteen MCP calls total. Unsupported catalogs fail deliberately; healthy ordinary/SDK-excluded controls execute, with zero stream producer starts. MCP returns invalid-params errors naming the unsupported command; actual JSON CLI configuration errors use the existing stdout logger and exit 1. The first after-probe incorrectly expects stderr; `/tmp/toolcraft-session112-stream-native-draft.log` and `/tmp/toolcraft-session112-stream-cli-diagnostic.log` preserve the fixture error. The corrected probe changes only its channel assertion, not production CLI routing. Final evidence: `/tmp/toolcraft-session112-stream-native.log` and inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-codemode-stream-after.png`.
- Maintained checks: selected 30-workspace codemode build closure and package lint/types pass. The first repository package-lint run rejects a stale root bundle after that dependency build (`poe-code/safe-fs`, `unpacked-canonical-output`); no publication rule is weakened. The maintained root-help rebuild refreshes 70 uncached tasks plus bundling, then all 17 package-lint rules and maintained root types pass. Root help is inspected. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session112-{build,lint,package-lint,root-help,package-lint-final,root-types}.log`. Snapshot 111 predates this focused repair; no newer full pass is claimed.

### TC-028: script console output contaminates MCP stdout

- Surface: codemode `execute`, especially the documented stdio-MCP wrapper; source: `packages/toolcraft-codemode/src/execute.ts`.
- Initial reproduction: execute `console.log("AUDIT_STDOUT_MARKER"); return 42;` using the default codemode SDK. Capturing `process.stdout.write` records `AUDIT_STDOUT_MARKER\n` even though execution returns a structured success result.
- Stdio reproduction: launch a local `runMCP(codeMode(root), ...)` subprocess, initialize it, and call its execute tool with the same synthetic script. Captured stdout contains a standalone non-JSON `AUDIT_STDIO_MARKER` line between JSON-RPC messages, followed by the valid tool result. The subprocess is terminated after the probe; no files or remote services are used.
- Expected: default script logging must not write unrelated bytes to the transport's protocol stream. SDK/CLI/MCP diagnostics should govern where logs are delivered.
- Cause: `execute` omitted the SafeJS sink unless a custom one was supplied, so SafeJS defaulted to the host console. The stdio server writes its protocol messages to that same stdout stream.
- Local remediation: default console log/error calls emit info/error runtime diagnostics, with normal argument formatting. Explicit low-level `makeExecuteCommand({ sink })` overrides remain supported unchanged.
- TDD evidence: the new diagnostic-routing test initially received no diagnostic events and showed the messages on the host console. It now passes, alongside a custom-sink compatibility test.
- Scope: default info messages obey the outer command runtime's configured log level; the default logger may suppress them. Callers that explicitly supply a sink remain responsible for that sink's output destination.
- Post-fix verification: the same real subprocess MCP call returns `42` with zero unframed stdout lines. A built-package CLI probe with info logging sends `script message` to stderr while stdout remains parseable JSON.

### TC-029: repeated references are mistaken for circular data

- Surfaces: shared HTTP-body redaction, including CLI/report diagnostics; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/redaction.ts`, `redactSecretLikeFieldsValue`.
- Reproduce: assign `{ label: "visible", password: "AUDIT_SECRET" }` to `shared`, then call `redactHttpBody({ first: shared, second: shared })`. Repeat with a shared array in two properties.
- Observed before the fix: the first copy is rendered and redacted correctly; the second becomes `"[Circular]"`, despite the input containing no cycle.
- Expected: repeated references render their redacted content wherever they appear; only an ancestor reference in the current traversal is circular.
- Cause: a single WeakSet retains completed branches, conflating previously visited objects with active ancestors.
- Local fix: remove each object/array from the active set after traversing it. Inputs are not mutated. Actual object and array cycles still produce the marker and terminate.
- TDD evidence: two repeated-reference regressions failed, while two genuine-cycle controls passed. All four now pass in `packages/toolcraft/src/redaction.test.ts`; the complete Toolcraft package suite also passes.

### TC-030: Basic authorization is mislabeled in core reports

- Surface: core header redaction and rendered error-report HTTP transcripts; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/redaction.ts`, `redactHttpHeaderValue`.
- Reproduce: call `redactHttpHeaderValue("Authorization", "Basic AUDIT_SECRET")`, or render an `HttpError` carrying that request header.
- Observed: output is `"Bearer ****"`; the report therefore names an authentication scheme not used by the request.
- Expected: mask the credential without inventing a different scheme; retaining `Basic` or masking the complete header would both avoid this incorrect diagnosis.
- Impact: authentication troubleshooting can start from misleading evidence. The synthetic secret itself is masked; this finding is not a credential leak.
- Local fix: core Authorization/proxy-Authorization formatting keeps `Bearer ****` only for an existing `Bearer ` prefix and fully masks other values as `****`. It no longer invents a Bearer scheme. Three report regressions cover Basic, Digest, and opaque credentials; existing Bearer report/CLI controls still pass.
- Verification: actual source-level verbose CLI capture masks Basic authorization without mislabeling it. OpenAPI's existing header presentation remains unchanged.

### TC-031: custom serialization bypasses report body redaction

- Surface: core `renderErrorReport` HTTP bodies; sources: `packages/toolcraft/src/redaction.ts` and `packages/toolcraft/src/error-report.ts`, `formatBody`/`stableJson`.
- Reproduce: create an `HttpError` whose request body is `{ toJSON() { return { password: "AUDIT_SERIALIZATION_SECRET", name: "visible" }; } }`; render with `{ error, version: "audit", env: {} }`.
- Observed: the exact synthetic password appears in the rendered report, even though a plain object with a password field is masked.
- Cause: the redactor copies the callable `toJSON` property. Subsequent JSON serialization invokes it after redaction and emits new, unredacted fields.
- Expected: serialization must not introduce secret-like fields after the last redaction step. Preserve supported JSON serialization semantics while checking the actual representation that will be emitted.
- Impact: a handler/library using a custom serializable request object can disclose fields users expect reports to mask. No production incident is asserted.
- Local fix: the shared redactor applies a value's `toJSON` hook before traversing the resulting representation, with the original receiver/key. It removes callable hooks from the redacted result so later formatting cannot reintroduce secret fields. Returned representations do not get their own same-level hook invoked again.
- Regression coverage: ordinary custom objects, self-returning hooks, array/function hooks, cycles through hook results, hook call counts, and whole-report masking in both copies of a request body. Date representations also remain ISO strings rather than becoming empty objects.
- Scope: the same shared body helper protects core CLI/API-summary/report paths; OpenAPI keeps its serialize-first transcript path. This is not a claim of complete JSON serialization conformance for every exotic object or arbitrary-text credential detection. Throwing getters/hooks and other unsupported diagnostic values remain audit leads.

### TC-032: cyclic error context breaks reporting

- Surface: `renderErrorReport`; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/error-report.ts`, `redactStructuredErrorField` and `formatStackChain`.
- Reproduce structured data: set `error.details = {}` and `error.details.self = error.details`, then render the error.
- Observed: report generation throws `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`, replacing the attempted diagnostic artifact with a reporting failure.
- Reproduce cause traversal safely: define an Error's `cause` getter to return that same Error for five reads and throw a sentinel on the sixth. Rendering reaches the sixth read and throws the sentinel instead of recognizing the repeated cause.
- Source evidence: structured-field recursion has no ancestor tracking; stack-chain formatting follows causes without a visited set.
- Expected: bounded rendering with a circular marker, preserving useful original error details and unique stack/cause entries.
- Local fix: structured fields first pass through the existing cycle-aware shared redactor; the report then applies header/known-string formatting to that safe structure. Stack-chain rendering separately tracks Error identities and emits `Caused by: [Circular]` instead of following a repeated cause.
- Regression evidence: object/array cycles, repeated non-circular references, secret masking within repeated data, and a bounded self-cause getter all pass. The cause getter is read once after the fix. Original input references remain unchanged.
- Boundary: no production hang or unbounded live test was performed. This closes the reproduced error-field/cause traversals, not every possible cycle in command schemas, parsed params, or arbitrary accessors.

### TC-033: typed HTTP errors omit the report transcript

- Surface: `renderErrorReport` HTTP transcript section; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/error-report.ts`, `hasHttpContext`.
- Reproduce: render the same request/response arguments using `new HttpError(args)` and `createHttpError(args)`, for statuses 400, 401, 429, 500, and 599.
- Observed: the base error includes an `HTTP Transcript` section. `BadRequestError`, `AuthenticationError`, `RateLimitError`, `InternalServerError`, and `ServerError` all omit it.
- Cause: HTTP context detection requires `error.name === "HttpError"`, excluding the package's own typed subclasses even when their context is identical.
- Expected: report formatting should recognize the supported HTTP error hierarchy/shape rather than a single exact display name.
- Impact: inconsistent report readability, not total loss of HTTP data. The same runtime comparison confirms status/request/response still appear among structured fields.
- Local fix: report rendering now reuses the existing API-summary structural HTTP-error detector instead of checking an exact name or local class identity. The existing `hasHttpContext` export is retained as an alias, not a proxy function.
- Regression evidence: the five typed status cases pass. An additional compatible typed Error that does not inherit the local HttpError class initially failed an intermediate class-based fix; it now also receives a transcript. Routine UserError report suppression and existing base-error fixtures remain passing controls.

### TC-034: failures in retry infrastructure trigger another attempt

- Surface: OpenAPI request retries; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/http.ts`, `fetchWithRetries`.
- Reproduce backoff failure: fake fetch returns 503 then successful JSON; configure two retries and a custom `sleep` that rejects once with `Error("backoff failed")`, then resolves.
- Reproduce diagnostic failure: use the same responses and a diagnostic logger that throws once on the first `Retrying` event, then accepts later events.
- Observed before the fix: both operations return the later successful JSON instead of rejecting with the local failure. The backoff failure is retried as if it were a network failure; the logger failure similarly triggers another retry diagnostic and request.
- Expected: only fetch failures enter the fetch retry branch. Retry infrastructure failures must not silently schedule extra network attempts or hide the supplied failure.
- Cause: the original try/catch encloses fetch, response classification, logging, and sleep.
- Local fix: limit that catch to the fetch call, with status retry cleanup/logging/sleep outside it. Network-error retries remain in the catch and existing retry tests still pass.
- TDD evidence: both regressions resolved unexpectedly before the fix; afterward they reject with the same supplied Error, make one fetch attempt, and cancel the discarded response. Sleep runs once for the sleep failure and not at all for the logger failure.
- Scope: synthetic fetch and in-memory diagnostic/sleep hooks only. No additional real network request was sent.

### TC-035: OpenAPI source diagnostics disclose credentials

- Surfaces: public `inspectOpenApiSource`, source loading/parsing, and shared spec-fetch status errors; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/spec-source.ts`.
- Reproduce through the public package: inspect `https://api.example.test/spec?api_key=AUDIT_SOURCE_SECRET` with fake fetch responses for 304, 404, 500, and 200. Use no body for 304, JSON `{ password: "AUDIT_SOURCE_BODY_SECRET", message: "failed" }` for errors, and JSON `null` for the 200 parse-shape failure.
- Observed: all four thrown messages contain the complete query credential. The 404 and 500 messages also include the unredacted password from the structured error-body snippet.
- Expected: source-status, source-label, and parse-error diagnostics apply the same credential masking as network-error diagnostics without changing the URL used to fetch the document.
- Cause: status errors stringify the raw URL and truncate the raw body text; `formatSourceLabel` returns source strings unchanged. Transport-error and response-read-error paths already use redacted URLs, making protection depend on the failure mode.
- Impact: a spec inspection/generation failure can disclose credentials even without enabling trace logging. This is a separate source-loader path from the request execution diagnostics fixed in TC-017 through TC-019.
- Local fix: generated status messages and source labels use the existing query redactor; recognizable JSON body snippets use the shared body redactor before truncation. Fetch URLs and caller-owned URL objects remain unchanged. Local path labels still use their original strings.
- TDD evidence: eight cases failed before the change and pass afterward, covering 304/404/500, invalid document shape, malformed YAML, an unclassified fetch failure, and long/nested JSON/problem-JSON credential fields. Long secret values are masked before the length cap, retaining useful error context that previously fell beyond the truncated prefix.
- Verification: 513 OpenAPI package tests and applicable lint/typechecks pass; source-level CLI status/parser screenshots show masked URLs/body fields and useful parser pointers. Reproductions used in-memory fake fetch, with no remote requests.
- Boundary: ordinary HTML/plain-text error snippets need a separately bounded policy; this finding demonstrates recognizable JSON secret fields, not arbitrary-text detection or protection for every credential-bearing URL component.

### TC-036: structured error fields only mask string-valued secrets

- Surface: the structured-fields section of `renderErrorReport`; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/error-report.ts`, `redactStructuredErrorField`.
- Reproduce: render an `HttpError` with request body `{ password: { value: "AUDIT_STRUCTURED_SECRET" }, access_token: 123456789 }`, ordinary empty headers, and a synthetic 400 response.
- Observed: both the exact nested secret marker and the numeric token appear in the report's structured-fields section. Neither appears in the same report's HTTP transcript.
- Expected: a recognized secret field is masked regardless of whether its value is a string, number, array, or object. Redacting only one of two copies in the report does not protect the artifact.
- Cause: this report-specific traversal checks sensitive names only inside its string branch. It traverses object-valued secrets without retaining the sensitive parent classification, and returns numeric values unchanged. Core `redactHttpBody` already handles these cases correctly.
- Impact: inconsistent duplicated redaction policies expose secret data in an otherwise apparently redacted report. This does not rely on custom serialization and is separate from TC-031.
- Local fix: structured fields use the shared secret-like-field redactor before report-specific header and declared-value formatting, removing the separate string-only masking policy.
- Regression evidence: number/object/array/boolean/null-valued secret fields are masked in both report copies, while public labels and original inputs remain intact. Existing string-valued secret and `secret: false` parameter controls still pass.
- Verification: the package suite and an actual source-rendered report/verbose CLI capture pass. The original reproduction used only in-memory synthetic data; no actual credential or report file was created.

### TC-037: parser diagnostics repeat source context

- Surface: OpenAPI source parsing errors and their CLI rendering; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/spec-source.ts`, `formatParseErrorMessage`.
- Reproduce: inspect a fake successful HTTP response containing `[invalid`, or run the source-level `audit inspect-parse` fixture used for the redaction screenshot.
- Observed: the error contains the YAML parser's own source line/caret and location, an additional parenthesized location, and Toolcraft's own source-snippet block for the same line/caret. The duplicate blocks are visible in `screenshots/toolcraft-spec-parse-redaction.png`.
- Expected UX: one clear location and one useful source excerpt, not two renderers repeating the same context. This is a confirmed presentation/usability issue rather than a claimed parsing failure.
- Cause: a preformatted parser message is combined with an independently rendered snippet. The extra position check also looks for `at line 1 column 9`, which does not match the parser's comma-separated `at line 1, column 9` wording.
- Remediation: request raw YAML diagnostics with `prettyErrors: false`, then use the existing structured positions/offsets and shared source renderer once. Removed the preformatted-message substring check. Positionless conversion errors remain plain messages; no fabricated line or snippet is added.
- Regression: session 77 initially reproduces 24 failures / 12 controls in `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/spec-source-presentation.test.ts`. The final matrix covers direct parsing and inspection, six syntax errors, LF/CRLF, positionless aliases, invalid top-level shapes, and valid JSON/YAML.
- Verification: four focused files / 124 tests, all 32 OpenAPI files / 859 tests, focused ESLint/types, and the selected 19-workspace build pass. Public inspection passes 36 cases on each exact Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0, and 22.23.2 (108 total). Inspected actual CLI diagnostics in `screenshots/toolcraft-spec-parse-presentation.png`; all three invalid fixtures retain exit one with one location line and one source excerpt.
- Status: locally fixed for diagnostic presentation. This does not change parsing policy or reopen TC-035. Full snapshot 75 predates this patch.

### TC-038: Unicode length constraints disagree across schema representations

- Surfaces: shared schema validation/default construction, SDK, MCP, and CLI; sources: `packages/toolcraft-schema/src/validate.ts`, `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts`, `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts`, and the CLI's string constraint paths.
- Reproduce minimum: `S.String({ minLength: 2 })` with `"😀"`. Shared `validate` accepts it; SDK, MCP `tools/call`, and CLI `--text` all invoke their handlers. The package's own `compileJsonSchema(toJsonSchema(schema)).validate` rejects the same value.
- Reproduce maximum: `S.String({ maxLength: 1 })` with the same input. Shared validation and all three invocation surfaces reject it, reporting string length two, while the compiled exported JSON Schema accepts it. CLI exits one and MCP returns `-32602`.
- Default construction also disagrees: `minLength: 2, default: "😀"` is accepted; `maxLength: 1, default: "😀"` throws at construction.
- Cause: the builder/runtime/command validators compare JavaScript `value.length` (UTF-16 code units). The existing JSON Schema evaluator instead uses `unicodeLength`, so a surrogate pair counts once there.
- Expected: exported schemas and actual invocation/default validation agree on Unicode code-point length. The existing `packages/toolcraft-schema/src/json-schema/utils.ts` helper provides a local implementation to evaluate for reuse; do not introduce an unrelated grapheme-counting rule.
- Control: decomposed `"e\u0301"` is accepted by both validators and all surfaces for `minLength: 2`. This distinguishes code points from user-perceived graphemes and prevents a misleading fix based on rendered glyph count.
- Impact: international text can be rejected despite satisfying a published schema, or accepted despite violating it. No security exploit is asserted.
- Local fix: reuse the existing `unicodeLength` implementation in schema validation, SDK, MCP, and both CLI flag/preset string-validation paths. Default builders inherit the corrected shared validator. Diagnostics now report the same code-point count used for the decision.
- TDD: 22 failures across the new runtime/default/preset checks were reproduced before implementation, with 199 passing existing/control cases. All 221 checks in that focused run now pass. Coverage includes astral characters, mixed astral/ASCII strings, combining marks, an unpaired surrogate, ASCII, empty strings, and rejected-input handler suppression.
- Verification: 2,302 schema-package tests and 734 Toolcraft-package tests passed, with package lint/typechecks; later default-compatibility additions have a separate verification milestone. Actual source-level CLI output is captured in `screenshots/toolcraft-unicode-length.png`. Error text/counts, usage pointers, exit codes, and handler counts are readable and verified. The screenshot font lacks the emoji glyph and shows a missing-glyph box; this image does not establish emoji rendering fidelity. The in-memory strings and validation assertions use the real astral character.
- Status: locally fixed for the covered surfaces. The original reproduction also used built public APIs and actual CLI/in-memory MCP dispatch. The comparison uses Toolcraft's own JSON Schema compiler, not a recalled external standard or a third-party validator assumption.

### TC-039: mutated defaults bypass invocation validation

- Surfaces: SDK and MCP missing-value default paths; sources: `packages/toolcraft/src/sdk.ts` and `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts`, `validateObjectSchema`.
- Reproduce: construct `field = S.Number({ default: 2, maximum: 10 })`, then set `field.default = 20`. Use `S.Object({ count: S.Optional(field) })` for a command and invoke it with `{}` through the SDK and an initialized MCP session.
- Observed: both handlers execute with `{ count: 20 }`; the MCP call returns a successful tool result. Shared `validate(params, {})` rejects the same missing-input/default combination with an issue at `count` for the maximum constraint.
- Expected: defaults applied at invocation should not bypass constraints enforced for supplied values. Report an invalid default before handler effects rather than silently injecting an out-of-range value.
- Cause: each surface's missing-value path assigns the descriptor default and continues without validating that value. Cloning alone addresses state aliasing (TC-006), not this validation gap.
- Remediation: validate applied defaults in their canonical schema context, retaining useful default/parameter paths. Do not pass schema-native object keys blindly through caller-casing normalization.
- Compatibility control: an existing SDK object default with canonical `visible_name` and a schema-scoped `hidden_name` currently delivers both default fields even after SDK scope filtering. A validation fix must explicitly resolve this behavior rather than accidentally reject previously usable defaults against a filtered schema. This observation is not itself classified as a defect.
- Additional repair boundary: shared validation normally applies nested optional defaults. A source-only control starts with the object default `{ visible_name: "present" }` and an absent optional numeric child with default 2; `validate` returns a new value containing that child. After the child's unused default is mutated to 20 above its maximum 10, validating the unchanged outer default rejects. Blindly reusing default-applying validation would therefore introduce or reject a value the current applied object default does not deliver. A fix must distinguish validating the actual canonical default payload from recursively applying additional defaults, while retaining isolation and useful error paths. Evidence: `/tmp/toolcraft-session115-default-validation-source-control.log`; this narrows the repair design and is not a separate product finding.
- Fresh public baseline: exact Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0, and 22.23.2 each run 54 ordinary/streaming SDK and snake/camel MCP cases. Six invalid-default scenarios cover optional/required numeric maxima, Unicode minimum, enum membership, array item type, and a canonical object member. Across runtimes, 108 invalid defaults reach handlers while 54 controls retain supplied overrides, canonical/scoped object contents, and unused nested defaults. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session116-defaults-<version>-before.log`. These expected defect observations are not successful validation of the invalid values.
- TDD: two new suites first report 298 failures / 63 controls (361 cases), all assertion failures rather than setup/import errors. Shared schema tests require a `defaults: "none"` policy; adapter cases require invalid defaults to fail before normal/streaming handlers. The regression matrix covers every descriptor kind, numeric/string/array constraints, JSON values, oneOf/union selection, optional and required fields, explicit undefined, nested objects, optional array/record items, and six adapter/casing/stream combinations. Log: `/tmp/toolcraft-session116-defaults-red.log`.
- Local repair: shared validation adds `defaults: "none"`, leaving the existing optional/all policies unchanged. Scope projections retain original schema provenance in a weak map. `packages/toolcraft/src/applied-default.ts` clones the applied value, validates it against the original canonical schema without adding defaults, reports its first schema issue, and returns the original cloned payload rather than a newly normalized value. SDK/MCP missing-member and optional undefined paths share this operation. This retains scoped/canonical fields, explicit undefined members, opaque permitted extras, and per-call data isolation; unused defaults are not validated.
- Additional controls cover scoped object defaults inside arrays, records, oneOf and unions, repeated projection provenance, and optional-requiredness promotion without changing a source node's identity. These bring new regression/control checks to 387: 31 shared-schema cases, 354 adapter cases and two projection controls. The initial focused seven-suite green run passes 649 tests; the final full package routes pass 14 schema files / 2,566 tests and 103 Toolcraft files / 7,194 tests. Selected build closure passes nineteen builds / 71 declared workspaces / 210 edges / eleven layers, and both maintained package lint/type routes pass. Handle 7329 is terminal exit zero; logs use `/tmp/toolcraft-session116-*.log`.
- Exact-runtime after verification repeats all 162 public observations: all 108 invalid applied defaults now reject (SDK UserError / MCP invalid parameters -32602), with zero handler calls, and all 54 compatibility controls remain unchanged. Logs: `/tmp/toolcraft-session116-defaults-<version>-after.log`. Six additional real CLI invocations expose the SDK error or a valid explicit override, asserting status, JSON output and handler counts. Inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-applied-defaults-after.png` shows the actual diagnostic and override result. This is CLI presentation of an SDK call, not a claim that TC-096's separate CLI object-container behavior is repaired.
- Public option types compile through both root and schema exports; the maintained published-consumer fixture now includes `defaults: "none"`. Session 117’s fresh packed-consumer smoke passes; the broader sequence is terminal with the separately documented committed-lock qualification failure. The non-README guide `docs/toolcraft-validation.md` documents all three policies and the applied-default compatibility boundary.
- Status: locally fixed for the confirmed SDK/MCP invocation paths. All probes use synthetic data; no external operation or LLM runs. Ordinary invalid defaults supplied directly to a builder remain rejected at construction and are not counted as this issue. Session 116 postdates snapshot 115; broader verification is required and no newer successful full check is claimed.

### TC-040: callable extras make accepted defaults crash shared validation

- Surface: shared schema validation; source: `packages/toolcraft-schema/src/validate.ts`, optional-default cloning.
- Reproduce: build `S.Object({ settings: S.Optional(S.Object({ items: S.Array(S.String()) }, { additionalProperties: true, default: { items: [], callback: () => 42 } })) })`, then call `validate(schema, {})`.
- Observed before the fix: construction succeeds, but applying the default throws `DataCloneError` instead of returning a validation result. The SDK previously delivered the callback and allowed it to run, so blindly adopting `structuredClone` there would introduce a compatibility regression.
- Expected: accepted callable extras should retain their callable identity while their containing ordinary data receives an independent copy. This does not promise independent closure state.
- Local fix: the validator retains native default cloning and falls back to `cloneDefaultValue` only for `DataCloneError`; other failures propagate. SDK/MCP argument defaults use that shared plain-copy helper directly to preserve their existing opaque-resource behavior. Copies preserve plain cycles, sparse arrays, null prototypes, and own `__proto__` data safely; opaque resources are retained by reference.
- TDD evidence: the public-validator regression failed with `DataCloneError` before implementation and now passes. Added controls cover native cloneable graphs/repeated references, fallback cycles/prototype-safe keys, opaque resources, and throwing getters. Getter exceptions are not silently treated as cloning failures.
- Status: locally fixed for the stated compatibility boundary. Default constraint validation (TC-039) is independently fixed in session 116, with callable-extra compatibility preserved.

### TC-041: exhausted scoped branches prevent MCP server construction

- Surfaces: schema scope projection and MCP server startup; sources: `packages/toolcraft/src/schema-scope.ts`, `filterSchemaForScope`, and `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts`, tool enumeration/registration.
- Reproduce: define an MCP-visible command whose `payload` is `S.OneOf({ discriminator: "kind", branches: { hidden: S.Object({ value: S.String() }, { scope: ["cli"] }) } })`. The public builder accepts this definition. Create an MCP server containing this command and a separate healthy MCP command.
- Observed: filtering removes every branch but leaves the outer union. Server construction throws `schema is invalid: oneOf must be a non-empty array of schemas.` The healthy sibling cannot be served because construction never completes. A non-discriminated `S.Union` with all branches scoped to CLI produces the same startup failure.
- Expected: scope projection must have a defined exhausted-branch policy consistent with other containers, and must not silently manufacture an invalid JSON Schema. If this configuration is forbidden, report its command/parameter context at the projection boundary rather than an unlocated downstream compiler failure.
- Controls: adding a visible branch allows server construction and successful calls; putting the scope on the entire parameter also allows construction. The latter exposed the separate invocation bug TC-042 and is not claimed as a successful call to that affected command.
- Historical related evidence: the initial probe also found SDK acceptance of hidden union payloads and acceptance of `{ payload: {} }` after scope filtering collapsed distinct required-key sets into two empty branches. These were recorded under TC-002/TC-004, whose later exact-one/discriminator fixes supersede those acceptance observations. They are not claims that the current matcher still accepts those inputs. Session 76 independently reconfirmed exhausted-branch construction failure before repairing it.
- Remediation: `filterSchemaForScope` now omits nonnullable exhausted union/discriminator nodes, matching existing exhausted array/record behavior. Optional/array/record parents propagate exclusion. Visible empty-object branches and nullable null-only residuals remain present; the original descriptor is unchanged.
- Pre-fix nullable control: with explicit schema-descriptor `nullable: true`, an otherwise exhausted union/discriminator produced a valid null-only `oneOf` branch and accepted `{ payload: null }`, while the equivalent nonnullable descriptor failed server construction. The repair preserves the valid nullable residual and removes the exhausted nonnullable field; it does not treat every empty canonical branch collection identically.
- Regression: `packages/toolcraft/src/schema-scope-exhausted.test.ts` initially has 38 failures / 102 passing controls before the repair; the final 172-case matrix adds 32 stream controls after the initial red run. It covers all three projection scopes, both variant kinds, direct/optional/array/record placement, visible branches, null-only residuals, and SDK/MCP normal/stream execution with healthy siblings. Actual CLI parameter projection remains TC-060, not resolved by helper-level CLI-scope checks.
- Verification: session 76 passes five focused files / 354 tests, all 93 Toolcraft files / 4,672 tests, package lint/types, and the selected 18-workspace build. Rebuilt public SDK/MCP checks pass 64 cases on each exact Node 18.18.0, 20.0.0, and 22.23.2 (192 total). Inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-scoped-union-projection.png`, captured from a real local CLI fixture exercising MCP discovery and calls.
- Status: locally fixed. All construction/invocation probes were local and synthetic; no external operation or production outage is asserted. Full snapshot 75 predates this patch.

### TC-042: MCP advertised scope/requiredness differs from executed validation

- Surface: MCP discovery and normal tool calls; sources: `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts`, `enumerateTools`, `validateToolArguments`, and normal/stream invocation sites.
- Reproduce: an MCP-visible command has `visibleName: S.String()`, `cliOnly: S.String({ scope: ["cli"] })`, and `requiredMcp: S.Optional(S.String({ requiredScopes: ["mcp"] }))`.
- Discovery correctly advertises only `visible_name` and `required_mcp`, both required, with `additionalProperties: false`.
- Observed call one: `{ visible_name: "sample", required_mcp: "present" }` follows discovery but fails with MCP `-32602`, `Missing required parameter "cli_only".` The hidden field is incorrectly required at execution.
- Observed call two: `{ visible_name: "sample", cli_only: "hidden" }` both supplies an unadvertised field and omits an advertised required field, yet succeeds and invokes the handler with canonical `{ visibleName: "sample", cliOnly: "hidden" }`.
- Cause: enumeration creates a scoped parameter schema for discovery, but normal invocation calls `validateToolArguments(tool.command.params, ...)` using the original command schema. The stream invocation site also passes the original schema.
- Stream reproduction: repeated discovery/subscription calls with both snake and camel casing. Discovery-conforming input fails for the hidden required field; input containing the hidden field while omitting the advertised required field successfully subscribes. Awaiting the data notification proves the generator ran with the hidden value and without the required value. Closing the session cleans up each synthetic stream. Stream failures use internal-error code `-32603`, independently registered as TC-043.
- HTTP reproduction: started the real Toolcraft HTTP MCP transport on an ephemeral loopback port, initialized a client, fetched discovery, and posted the same two calls. Discovery-conforming input produced protocol `-32602`; hidden-field/missing-required input executed successfully. Both responses used HTTP 200 with their respective JSON-RPC error/result bodies, as expected for this protocol layer. The listener was closed in `finally`; no remote endpoint or credentials were used.
- Expected: discovery and execution use the same projected scope and requiredness rules. Hidden fields must not become secret required inputs, and fields promoted by `requiredScopes` must be required before handler execution.
- Remediation: retain the filtered object schema in the internal tool definition and use it for every argument-validation path. Cover normal calls, streams, nested scope filtering, both casing modes, and `requiredScopes`; do not change command-wide authorization assumptions.
- Local fix: retain `paramsSchema` on the internal tool definition and use that exact projected descriptor for normal calls and subscriptions. The original command definition remains available for handler metadata and requirements; no caller-facing option was added.
- TDD: 28 failures / eight passing controls before implementation; all 36 cases now pass. The matrix covers normal/stream calls, snake/camel casing, hidden required/optional fields, nested objects/array items, `requiredScopes`, hidden field defaults, visible fields, and another surface's requiredness. Each case also checks the advertised JSON Schema's decision and the canonical handler payload.
- HTTP verification: repeated the real loopback transport calls after the patch. Discovery-conforming input executes once with only the expected canonical fields. Hidden-field/missing-required input is rejected; a separate hidden-field-only call also fails before its handler. Actual JSON-RPC responses are captured in `screenshots/toolcraft-mcp-scope-wire.png` and were visually inspected. This is a wire-response capture, not a claim about interactive CLI layout.
- Status: locally fixed for projected parameter validation. This is distinct from codemode's analogous discovery mismatch (TC-025), which remains open. Union/discriminator matching and exhausted-branch projection have separate local fixes under TC-002–TC-004 and TC-041.

### TC-043: stream subscription errors lose classification and metadata

- Surface: MCP stream subscription; sources: `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts`, the `toolcraft/streams/subscribe` method and `toToolError`.
- Reproduce: define a normal command and a stream command with identical `S.Object({ target: S.String() })` params. Initialize a local MCP session and pass `{ target: 42 }` to `tools/call` and `toolcraft/streams/subscribe` respectively.
- Observed: both report `Invalid value for "target". Expected a string, got 42.` The normal command returns `-32602` (invalid params), but the stream method returns `-32603` (internal error). Neither handler needs to run to reproduce the mismatch.
- Expected: caller-correctable parameter errors keep their invalid-input classification across invocation styles. Clients should not have to parse human-readable messages to distinguish a malformed subscription from a server defect.
- Additional reproduction: a requirement throws `ToolError(-32042, "Requirement protocol error.", { retryable: false })`. Normal invocation retains its code/data; subscription changes the code to `-32603` and drops the data. Direct sync/async custom-method tests reproduce this in the underlying transport without Toolcraft.
- Cause: two boundaries are involved. Toolcraft subscription validation throws `UserError` without its usual mapper, and `packages/tiny-stdio-mcp-server/src/server.ts` treats every custom-method exception as internal, including an explicit `ToolError` that normal tool calls preserve.
- Remediation: consistently map pre-subscription validation/requirement errors while preserving genuine internal errors and any existing protocol error objects. Check missing/unknown/incorrectly typed params and unknown stream identifiers; keep post-start stream error notifications a separate lifecycle concern.
- Local fix: wrap subscription errors with the existing Toolcraft mapper, and preserve explicit `ToolError` code/message/defined data at the generic custom-method transport boundary. Ordinary exceptions remain internal errors. Post-start notification behavior and unsubscribe semantics are unchanged.
- TDD: seven failures / two passing controls in the nine Toolcraft subscription cases; six failures / 288 passing existing/control cases in the underlying transport file. After the two fixes and maintained two-workspace dependency build, all 339 tests in the focused three-file run pass. Coverage includes missing/wrong/extra params, missing/unknown streams, ordinary requirement errors, typed errors, falsy metadata, sync/async method handlers, and successful data delivery.
- Verification: 80 files / 1,916 tests across Toolcraft, the stdio transport, and the HTTP transport pass; applicable Toolcraft lint and focused transport ESLint/TypeScript checks pass. Loopback HTTP responses retain `-32602` for invalid input and `-32042` plus `{ retryable: false }` for the typed control. The inspected `screenshots/toolcraft-mcp-stream-errors-wire.png` captures these actual JSON-RPC bodies.
- Status: locally fixed for pre-subscription and custom-method exception boundaries. No remote stream was opened; local listeners and message sessions were closed.

### TC-044: cyclic causes hang network-error classification

- Surface: OpenAPI network diagnostics; source: `packages/toolcraft-openapi/src/network-error.ts`, `findNetworkError` and `findAbortError`, used by HTTP request/source failure handling.
- Reproduce first loop: create `error = new Error("cycle")`, set `error.cause = error`, then call `classifyNetworkError(error, "https://api.example.test")`. The network-code search never finishes.
- Reproduce second loop: add own `code: "UNCLASSIFIED"` to the same cyclic error. The first search returns this code, but the subsequent abort-error search never finishes.
- Concrete bounded evidence: each case ran in its own child process, printed `READY` immediately before classification, and did not return before the parent's one-second timeout terminated that child. An acyclic control printed `RETURNED null` and exited zero. This distinguishes time spent inside classification from startup/import failure; no unbounded live hang was left running.
- Expected: visit each cause object at most once; return the best available classification or preserve the original unclassified failure without blocking the event loop.
- Cause: both cause walkers repeatedly follow own `cause` properties without cycle tracking. The core error-report cycle fix TC-032 does not cover these separate OpenAPI diagnostic walkers.
- Remediation: use per-traversal identity tracking, preserve recognized codes/abort errors encountered before a repeated node, and add fast getter-guarded cycle tests rather than hanging unit tests or waiting for test timeouts.
- Local fix: both cause walkers track visited object identities independently and stop at a repeated cause. Recognized network/abort classifications encountered before a repeat are retained.
- TDD: four guarded cycle cases failed before the implementation, while 16 existing/control cases passed. The guard makes repeated traversal throw immediately instead of hanging the unit runner. All 20 classifier tests now pass, including self/pair cycles, an unknown-code cycle that reaches the second walker, a cyclic abort error, and a recognized network code before a repeated node.
- Verification: 25 OpenAPI files / 518 package tests passed. The package has no declared `lint` script; direct focused ESLint and package TypeScript checks passed. Actual source-level CLI execution with a cyclic fake-fetch failure returns exit one instead of hanging and is captured in `screenshots/toolcraft-network-cycle.png`; the image was inspected.
- Status: locally fixed for the documented cause traversals. The reproductions use synthetic error objects and do not assert that ordinary network responses themselves can construct cyclic JavaScript causes. Throwing arbitrary accessors and unrelated error walkers remain outside this fix.

### TC-045: external stream cancellation leaves rejected cleanup promises unobserved

- Surfaces: SDK managed streams, MCP stream pumping, and CLI SIGINT; sources: `packages/toolcraft/src/stream.ts`, `abortFromConsumer`, `close`, and the `getIterator` call in `next`; `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts`, detached subscription pump and its final cancellation; `packages/toolcraft/src/cli.ts`, interrupt callback.
- Cleanup reproduction: create a stream whose async generator yields `"ready"` and throws `Error("AUDIT_STREAM_CLEANUP_FAILURE")` in `finally`. Consume the first event, then abort the signal supplied to `sdk.watch({}, { signal })`. The external abort listener invokes `void close(...)`, and the rejected generator-return promise has no rejection handler.
- Concrete evidence: an isolated source-import Node process with an `unhandledRejection` observer receives exactly the cleanup error. A second isolated process without that observer prints `Before external abort`, reports the cleanup stack, and exits one before its next `setImmediate` continuation. Runtime: Node v22.23.2. No network, persistent fixture, or LLM call was involved.
- Startup variant: an async stream handler throws before returning an iterable. Catching the first `iterator.next()` rejection leaves `stream.signal.aborted === false` and one listener attached to the consumer signal, measured with `node:events`' `getEventListeners`. Aborting that signal later emits the already-handled startup error as an unhandled rejection. Iterator acquisition occurs outside `next`'s cleanup catch; closing then awaits the same rejected initialization promise.
- Additional MCP evidence: subscribe to a stream whose async handler throws before returning an iterable. The server successfully returns a subscription identifier and emits the expected error notification, then its detached pump's `finally { await stream.cancel(); }` emits the same startup failure as an unhandled rejection. A separate source-import process asserted both the notification and the exact unhandled error. This is the same startup/cleanup ownership defect, not a duplicate finding.
- Expected: terminal startup/cleanup failures have an observable stream-consumer error path, but an AbortSignal event callback must not create an unhandled rejected promise that can terminate the hosting process. Failed startup should detach its consumer listener and transition to a terminal state.
- Additional CLI evidence: while a custom iterator's `next()` is pending, injected SIGINT causes its failing `return()` to reject the interrupt callback's discarded cancellation promise. The CLI can later report the cleanup error when the pending pull is released, but the earlier unhandled rejection has already occurred. A dedicated regression failed before observing the interrupt promise.
- Local fix: pin the lazy creation/cleanup promises before reentrant callbacks, enter terminal state and detach the consumer listener during close, and handle failed iterator creation as having no iterator to return. Both pre-pull and post-pull cancellation checks suppress late values. SDK abort listeners and CLI interrupt handlers observe their detached cancellation promises; the MCP pump does not re-emit an already-reported cleanup rejection from its final drain. Explicitly awaited cancel/return failures remain catchable.
- TDD: ten failures / four passing controls in the initial lifecycle matrix, then a separate CLI SIGINT failure / 14 passing controls. All 15 final cases pass. Coverage includes synchronous/asynchronous initialization failure, cancellation during acquisition and a pending pull, reentrant cancellation, cancellation requested from creation, explicit cleanup failures, pre-pull cancellation, normal completion, and public SDK/MCP/CLI rejection ownership. An initial test's direct throw in `finally` failed lint; replaced it with the failing cleanup mock that the generator calls, without suppressing the rule.
- Verification: 63 Toolcraft files / 1,330 tests and package lint/typechecks pass. The maintained selected Toolcraft build completed 18 builds; consumer checks passed 46 files / 975 tests. Source subprocesses and rebuilt public APIs on Node 18.18.2 and 22.23.2 complete without an unhandled-rejection observer; explicit SDK cancellation failure still rejects with the original error. The inspected actual CLI screenshot is `screenshots/toolcraft-stream-lifecycle.png`, using synthetic SIGINT during a pending pull and asserting exactly one cleanup.
- Boundary: the failure probes deliberately inject failing application cleanup/startup. This is not a claim that arbitrary notification-sink failures are now contained, or that an uncooperative producer's pending work can be forcibly interrupted. Explicit cancellation errors remain observable; successful cancellation and normal completion retain controls. Status: locally fixed for the documented lifecycle paths.

### TC-046: MCP stream event casing disagrees with discovery

- Surface: MCP stream discovery, subscription response, and data notifications; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts`, `applySchemaCasing` and the subscription pump.
- Reproduce: declare `event: S.Object({ display_name: S.String(), detailList: S.Array(S.Object({ file_path: S.String() })) })` and emit `{ display_name: "Ada", detailList: [{ file_path: "sample" }] }`. Initialize an in-process MCP message session and subscribe in each supported casing mode.
- Observed with snake casing: the subscription advertises required `detail_list`, but emits `detailList`. With camel casing, it advertises `displayName` and nested `filePath`, but emits `display_name` and `file_path`. Both events fail the exact returned event schema when passed to the repository's JSON Schema compiler; the failures include missing advertised required fields and forbidden canonical extras.
- Expected: a valid handler event is serialized to the same casing that the server advertises. Normal command results already have a dedicated canonical-to-wire conversion path. SDK events should retain the SDK's established handler-value contract rather than receive an MCP-only conversion.
- Cause: discovery and subscription responses apply schema casing, but the stream pump inserts the raw `event` directly into its notification. Shared managed-stream validation checks the canonical event schema and does not convert wire keys.
- Impact: schema-driven clients reject valid emitted data or read undefined from the documented fields. This affects ordinary schema names in the default snake mode as well as explicit camel mode, including nested arrays/objects.
- Remediation: validate canonical events and serialize them through a result/event conversion path before emitting MCP data notifications. Cover top-level primitives/arrays, nested records, discriminators/unions, defaults, and both casing modes; assert against the advertised JSON Schema, not just hand-written expected objects. Do not introduce another first-key union selector.
- Local fix: serialize each canonical event through the result-value conversion path, then validate it against the exact advertised event schema before publishing data. The wire validator is compiled once per subscription. Validated tags, nested object/array/record fields, and defaults are normalized; record keys, opaque JSON, and additional properties keep their documented untyped form. SDK and CLI event contracts are unchanged.
- Necessary shared-path repairs: result unions now use exact-one selection (TC-004), result discriminators use own-property selection and authoritative wire tags, optional undefined object fields are omitted, and applied defaults are cloned and recursively serialized rather than inserted with canonical keys. Optional array values that satisfy the canonical Optional descriptor but cannot satisfy the advertised wire schema emit an error notification instead of malformed data.
- TDD: authoritative RED was 50 failures / 78 passing controls; all 128 final cases pass. The matrix covers normal results and streams in both casing modes, primitives, nullable objects, nested containers, records, additional properties, optional omission/defaults, array-item defaults, discriminators and wire aliases, prototype-sensitive branches, exact-one unions in both orders, invalid values, schema conformance, and handler-value immutability.
- Verification: 64 Toolcraft files / 1,458 tests and package lint/typechecks pass. The selected build completes 18 builds; 46 consumer files / 975 tests pass. Rebuilt MCP APIs pass union and defaulted-event probes in both casing modes on Node 18.18.2 and 22.23.2. `screenshots/toolcraft-mcp-output-contracts.png` captures inspected actual result/event payloads from in-process JSON-RPC sessions; it is not an interactive CLI or network-transport screenshot.
- Boundary/status: locally fixed for emitted data/schema agreement. Default metadata casing was separately reproduced and fixed under TC-050. No general arbitrary-JavaScript-to-JSON safety or notification-sink-failure guarantee is claimed. A nullable-container casing lead was not promoted: current builders preserve a scalar type with nullable metadata rather than the hypothesized array-valued type.

### TC-047: CLI approval and early-command outcomes bypass normal output routing

- Surface: CLI successful pending-approval output and declined-approval errors; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/cli.ts`, `renderHumanInLoopPending`, `renderApprovalDeclined`, and their special-case call sites.
- JSON-result reproduction: return a valid `HumanInLoopPending` object from a command and invoke it with `--output json`. Instead of serialized pending metadata, stdout contains a checkmark, `Queued for human approval`, and indented tracking text. The command exits zero, but `JSON.parse(stdout)` fails. An ordinary `{ ok: true }` result in the same mode is valid JSON.
- Capture reproduction: supply `runCLI(..., { outputEmitter })`. Ordinary successful results and ordinary `UserError` messages reach that callback. Pending and `ApprovalDeclinedError` outcomes produce an empty callback capture and write directly to process stdout instead. The declined case also falls outside the normal resolved-output context and produces rich decoration in the same JSON-mode probe.
- Expected: pending approval is a structured command outcome, so JSON result mode should preserve its machine-readable metadata. All these outcomes should respect the supplied output sink. Preserve rich approval wording in rich mode and exit one for a decline.
- Cause: pending results return through a direct `process.stdout.write` helper before `renderCLIResult`; declined errors return through `createLogger()` without the caller's emitter or the normal error-output context.
- Additional early-error reproduction: configure `outputEmitter` and invoke `helo` when only `hello` exists. The callback receives nothing, while decorated unknown-command text is written directly to stdout. Unknown-option and invalid-number controls instead reach the callback once with no global writes. All three exit one and execute no handler. `findUnknownCommanderCommand` returns through another unconfigured `createLogger()` branch before normal error routing. This extends the same output-capture defect beyond approval outcomes without imposing JSON encoding on every error.
- Evidence: four source CLI scenarios assert success, ordinary user error, pending, and decline behavior. Captured and visually inspected `/tmp/toolcraft-audit-approval-output.png`, showing actual CLI success/pending/decline output under `--output json`. The image is outside the repository to avoid changing the file set during the running full lint chain.
- Sink clarification: the earlier plain-text `UserError` observation used `outputEmitter`, whose logger contract receives the raw message. Without that callback, ordinary errors already use the design logger's JSON or Markdown formatting. Comparative regression tests now cover both sinks; the fix follows their existing behavior rather than imposing a new universal error envelope.
- Fix: rich pending output keeps its wording and uses the supplied emitter; JSON/Markdown/custom formats receive pending metadata through normal result rendering without calling a success-only command renderer. Declines and early unknown-command errors now enter the existing output-format context and error renderer, retaining exit one, suggestions, and usage-pointer rules. Declines still avoid unexpected-error reports.
- Regression evidence: the initial 17-case matrix failed 15 cases with two rich-output controls passing. Expanded coverage adds nested unknown commands, both output sinks, actual synchronous approved/declined provider outcomes, and preservation of approved-success renderers: 32 cases in `packages/toolcraft/src/cli-approval-output.test.ts`.
- Boundary: asynchronous pending output tests inject the runtime outcome to avoid process spawning; the real synchronous runtime uses an in-memory fake provider. No external approval UI or action is triggered. Custom CLI binaries/naming and unrelated diagnostic sinks are not claimed covered.

### TC-048: MCP approval tracking hint omits the required CLI flag

- Surface: MCP pending-approval content; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/mcp.ts`, `renderPendingApproval`, compared with `packages/toolcraft/src/human-in-loop/approvals-commands.ts`, `showParams`.
- Reproduce: receive a pending MCP outcome whose text says `Track with toolcraft approvals show <approval-id>`, then pass those exact command arguments to the generated CLI. It exits one with `Missing required parameter "approvalId"` and a usage pointer. The generated `show` command expects `--approval-id <approval-id>`, not a positional ID.
- Positive control: created a real approval record with `enqueueApproval` in an in-memory `memfs` task list, supplied its runtime to the generated CLI, and retried using `--approval-id`. The corrected form exits zero and returns the exact approval record as JSON. The fake provider is configured to throw if invoked and is never called; no action executes and no persistent task list is created.
- Cause: the MCP text hard-codes a positional argument while the command definition exposes an ordinary named parameter. The CLI's own pending-output helper already includes `--approval-id`, demonstrating the cross-surface inconsistency.
- Remediation: derive or align tracking syntax with the generated command contract, including configured command naming/casing where supported. Keep a round-trip check that the displayed instruction reaches the existing approval without missing-parameter errors. Do not treat adding `--yes` as a substitute for a missing required identifier.
- Fix: the MCP hint includes `--approval-id`, matching the generated command and CLI pending hint. `packages/toolcraft/src/approval-tracking.test.ts` obtains the actual MCP instruction and executes it against a real approval record in a `memfs` task list; the exact record is returned and remains pending, with no provider call. The existing MCP runtime expectation is updated to the corrected syntax.
- Evidence boundary: the new round-trip test initially omitted MCP scope; that fixture error was corrected before recording the meaningful red test (CLI exit one). The original manual diagnostic assertion expected an excess-argument error, but current CLI reports the missing required named parameter. The fix covers that confirmed flag mismatch, not arbitrary wrapper binaries or alternative naming conventions.

### TC-049: excess CLI arguments are silently discarded

- Surface: CLI argument validation; source: `packages/toolcraft/src/cli.ts`, `createNodeCommand`, `resolveParams`, and `parseDynamicValues`.
- Reproduce with no declared positionals: `audit check unexpected` exits zero and executes the handler with `{}`. With one declared `name` positional, `audit check expected unexpected` executes with `{ name: "expected" }`; the second word disappears without a diagnostic.
- Dynamic-field reproduction: declare `labels: S.Record(S.String())` and invoke `audit check --labels.env prod unexpected`. The handler executes with `{ labels: { env: "prod" } }`, again silently losing the last word.
- Positive controls: a declared variadic string-array positional receives both `one` and `two`; an unknown `--unexpected` option is rejected before its handler with a usage pointer. These controls separate unsupported excess words from intended variadic and dynamic-option values.
- Expected: reject unconsumed arguments before running a handler. A typo or mistaken positional form should not produce successful execution with a different argument set. Preserve variadic positionals and correctly consumed dynamic-option values.
- Cause: every leaf calls `allowExcessArguments(true)`. Parameter resolution consumes declared positionals but does not validate the remaining argv tokens; dynamic parsing skips tokens that do not start with `--`. The approval-hint investigation first exposed the missing-parameter symptom, but this independent reproduction supplies all required values and demonstrates actual handler execution.
- Fix: retain Commander's parsed unknown-option partition separately from true operands, consume dynamic option/value tokens once, and validate the remaining positional count before field resolution. Excess tokens produce a usage error without invoking the handler. Declared variadics remain unbounded, dynamic arrays still consume their own values, and explicit `--` boundaries are honored.
- Regression evidence: the initial combined TC-049/TC-058 matrix failed 40 of 60 cases, with 20 passing controls.
